6.2
Empirical null distribution

We examine the empirical distribution of
\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda})
under the null hypothesis. We consider five choices of the regularization parameter
\lambda
: two fixed values,
\lambda=0.5
and
\lambda=1
, and three data-driven values, namely the Bayes selections corresponding to
D\propto I_{p}
and
D\propto\Sigma_{p}
, and the minimax selection when the prior family consists of all linear combinations of
I_{p}
and
\Sigma_{p}
with nonnegative coefficients satisfying (
5.4
). See Section S.4.1 of the Supplementary Material for details. The latter three are denoted by
\hat{\lambda}_{I_{p}}
,
\hat{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{p}}
, and
\hat{\lambda}_{*}
. In addition, we report the test size of the procedure in
Han et al. (2016)
, when
p<n_{1}+n_{2}
, corresponding to
\lambda=0
. Table
6.1
reports the empirical sizes at the asymptotic
5\%
level. The results indicate that empirical sizes are consistently close to the nominal level, generally falling within the range of
3\%\text{--}6\%
, even with data-driven choices of
\lambda
.

In Section S.4.3 of the Supplementary Material, we report the empirical density of
\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda})
. The results show that the empirical distributions closely follow the Tracy–Widom law of type 1, both when normalized using the true values
\Theta_{1p}
and
\Theta_{2p}
and when using their estimators, thereby supporting the validity of the asymptotic theory and the accuracy of the proposed estimators. This pattern is consistent across all five choices of
\lambda
.

Fig 6.2:
Size-adjusted empirical power
\Sigma
is Poly-Decay. Columns (left to right) correspond to
\hat{\gamma}_{2}=0.3,0.5,0.9,2
; rows (top to bottom) correspond to
n_{1}=50,100,250
. Solid curves: blue (
\lambda=0.5
), orange (
\lambda=1
), black (
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}_{I_{p}}
), purple (
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{p}}
), and pink (
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}_{*}
). Dashed curves: red (Proj-LRT), yellow (Ridge-LRT), and green (
Han et al. (2016)
,
\lambda=0
), the latter available only when
p<n_{1}+n_{2}
.

6.3
Empirical power

In this section, we evaluate the empirical power of the proposed tests and compare them with Ridge-LRT, Proj-LRT, and
Han et al. (2016)
(
\lambda\to 0
). Figure
6.2
presents power curves against the signal strength parameter
\zeta
(See Section S.4.1 in the Supplementary Material) under the Poly-Decay setting; results for other settings are provided in Section S.3.3 of the Supplementary Material. To ensure a fair comparison, all power curves are adjusted using size-corrected cutoffs derived from each test’s empirical null distribution via simulation. Results of the proposed methods based on asymptotic cutoffs are similar and therefore omitted for brevity.

The proposed methods demonstrate substantially higher power across all simulation settings than the likelihood-based methods, Ridge-LRT and Proj-LRT, underscoring their effectiveness in detecting concentrated alternatives. The test of
Han et al. (2016)
exhibits comparable performance when
\hat{\gamma}_{2}=0.3
, but becomes noticeably less powerful as
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
increases, particularly when
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
approaches 1. This behavior is consistent with the theoretical power analysis in Sections
4.3
and
4.4
.

Under the alternative model in Setting (v) (See Section S.4.1 in the Supplementary Material), the matrix
BC
can be viewed as a generalization of the
PA
model (
5.2
) with
D=I_{p}
. Accordingly,
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}_{I_{p}}
corresponds to the data-driven Bayesian choice under a correctly specified prior, whereas
\hat{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{p}}
represents the choice under a mildly misspecified prior. The choice
\lambda=\hat{\lambda}_{I_{p}}
consistently attains the highest power among all considered settings as expected. Nevertheless, across all settings, the loss in power is relatively modest when
\lambda
is fixed at
0.5
,
1
or when the prior is mildly misspecified. In contrast, the minimax selection exhibits a noticeable efficiency loss, which is typical of minimax procedures that trade optimality under specific alternatives for robustness across a broader class of scenarios.

Lastly, the power of the largest root tests remains essentially stable as
n_{1}
varies, whereas increasing
n_{1}
leads to a noticeable decline in power for Proj-LRT and Ridge-LRT. This behavior arises because the variance of LRT–type statistics inflates when
p
is large, thereby degrading the signal-to-noise ratio. This highlights the advantage of the proposed methods in settings that involve the simultaneous testing of many linear hypotheses.

7
Discussion

In this paper, we proposed a powerful and computationally efficient largest-root test for high-dimensional general linear hypotheses, based on a ridge-regularized
F
-matrix. Leveraging tools from Random Matrix Theory, we established that, under the null hypothesis, the largest eigenvalue follows an asymptotic Tracy–Widom distribution after appropriate scaling, in regimes where the dimension is comparable to the effective sample size and the number of hypotheses. We developed a consistent and efficient procedure for estimating the scaling parameters, relying solely on the empirical eigenvalues of the test matrices and without requiring structural assumptions. The power of the test and the influence of the regularization parameter were analyzed under a class of rank-one alternatives. Methods for data-driven selection of the regularization parameter are developed, guided by Bayesian decision theory and minimaxity. Extensive simulation studies demonstrate that the proposed test maintains well-controlled type I error rates and exhibits strong power across diverse settings. Furthermore, the method is robust to variations in covariance structure and data distributions, underscoring its practical utility in high-dimensional hypothesis testing.

Several promising research directions can be pursued to extend the current work. From a technical standpoint, our analysis assumes regular stability properties for the leading eigenvalues of the population covariance matrix. This excludes important models such as classical factor models, where a finite number of eigenvalues are well-separated from the bulk. Investigation under a class of spiked covariance models is a valuable direction for future research. Another avenue is the integration of the proposed test with variable screening techniques to enable application in ultra-high-dimensional regimes, where
p
grows substantially faster than the sample sizes. By reducing dimensionality prior to testing, such an approach could improve computational efficiency while preserving statistical power.

,

, and

, additional simulation results, detailed proofs of the technical results presented in the paper, and possible extensions of the current framework both
technically and in terms of the form of the linear hypotheses.

for some given matrices
A
,

and

.

S.1
Real data analysis

The Human Connectome Project (HCP), funded by the National Institutes of Health (NIH), aims to map the neural pathways that underlie human brain function and behavior. A consortium led by Washington University in St. Louis and the University of Minnesota has collected extensive neuroimaging and behavioral data from
n_{0}=1,113
healthy young adults aged
22
to
35
. This dataset facilitates detailed analyses of brain circuits and their relationships to behavior and genetics. Among the publicly available data are cerebral volumetric measurements and various behavioral evaluation scores. In this section, we utilize the proposed tests to investigate the associations between cerebral measurements and behavioral variables using the HCP dataset.

The behavioral evaluation scores in the HCP dataset encompass several domains, including cognition, emotion, sensation, and motor functions. The cognition domain assesses various cognitive abilities such as episodic memory, cognitive flexibility, and attention control. The emotion domain includes measures of psychological well-being, social relationships, stress, and self-efficacy. The sensation domain comprises tests related to audition, olfaction, taste, and vision. The motor domain evaluates aspects like cardiovascular endurance, manual dexterity, grip strength, and gait speed. After a pre-screening process to filter out highly correlated variables, we selected
p=127
representative behavioral variables to study their associations with cerebral measurements.

Cerebral measurements were obtained for 68 cortical surface regions, assessing surface area, cortical thickness, and gray matter volume. These regions are distributed across 14 cerebral lobes symmetrically located in both hemispheres, resulting in a total of
204
cortical surface variables (
68
regions
\times

3
measurement types). Additionally, each voxel in the brain’s subcortical structures was assigned one of 44 labels, with the volume of each labeled region measured, yielding 44 subcortical variables. Four labels were excluded from the analysis due to a high proportion of missing values.

Available demographic information includes the age and gender of subjects. Specifically, the subjects are divided into four age groups, namely 22–25, 26–30, 31–35, and 36+. The data set is roughly balanced with respect to gender, as the 1113 subjects consist of 606 females and 507 males.

Specifically, we consider the following multivariate regression model:

Y_{i}=\beta_{0}+\beta_{1}^{T}\mathbf{L}_{1i}+\dots+\beta_{14}^{T}\mathbf{L}_{14,i}+\beta_{15}^{T}\mathbf{SC}_{i}+\beta_{16}^{T}\mathbf{D}_{i}+\Sigma_{p}^{1/2}Z_{i}.

(S.1.1)

where (i)
Y_{i}
is the vector of
127
behavioral scores of subject
i
; (ii)
\mathbf{L}_{ji}
(
j=1,\dots,14
) represents the cerebral measurements vector of the surface regions belonging to lobe
j
for subject
i
, with dimensions varying from
3
to
33
depending on the lobe. The variables are of 3 types: surface area, cortical thickness, and gray matter volume; (iii)
\mathbf{SC}_{i}
contains the volume measurements of
40
subcortical regions of subject
i
; (iv)
\mathbf{D}_{i}
are age and gender group dummy variables of subject
i
. This model allows us to assess the associations between various cerebral measurements and behavioral outcomes while accounting for demographic variables. The dimension of the explanatory variables (including the intercept) is
m=249
, the dimension of the response
Y_{i}
is
p=127
, and the sample size is
n_{0}=1113
. We are interested in testing the joint significance of all variables associated with each cerebral lobe. Namely, we test
H_{0}:\beta_{j}=0
against
H_{a}:\beta_{j}\neq 0
, for each
j=1,\dots,14
. The proposed test procedure is applied with five choices of
\lambda
:
0.5\bar{\tau}
,
\bar{\tau}
,
1.5\bar{\tau}
, the data-driven choice of
\lambda
described in Section
5
with
D=I_{p}
and when
D=\Sigma_{p}
. The latter two are referred to as
\hat{\lambda}_{I_{p}}
and
\hat{\lambda}_{\Sigma_{p}}
, respectively. Here,
\bar{\tau}
is the average of eigenvalues of
\mathbf{W}_{2}
. The p-values are reported in Table

Table S.1.1:
P-values: Joint significance tests of 14 lobes.

Among the significant coefficients at the 90% level detected by the proposed tests with at least one choice of
\lambda
are left medial temporal lobe, right frontal lobe, and left parietal lobe. Similar results are reported in the literature, for example
Li et al. (2024)
. The left medial temporal lobe, encompassing structures such as the hippocampus and parahippocampal gyrus, plays a pivotal role in memory formation and retrieval. Damage to the lobe has been associated with impairments in verbal memory tasks, indicating its importance in processing verbal material. Functional connectivity within the lobe is crucial for cognitive functions like episodic memory.

The frontal lobes, located directly behind the forehead, are the largest lobes in the human brain and are essential for voluntary movement, expressive language, and higher-level executive functions. These executive functions encompass a range of cognitive abilities, including planning, organizing, initiating, self-monitoring, and controlling responses to achieve specific goals. Damage to the frontal lobe can result in impairments in these areas, affecting one’s ability to perform tasks that require these skills.

The parietal lobe, situated behind the frontal lobe, plays a pivotal role in integrating sensory information from various parts of the body. It is responsible for processing touch, temperature, pressure, and pain sensations. Additionally, the left parietal lobe is involved in controlling skilled motor actions and is associated with numerical processing and language functions. Damage to this area can lead to difficulties in these domains, affecting one’s ability to perform tasks that require these skills.

S.2
Discussion and implementation details of the proposed estimation method

Discussion
. Additional constraints can be incorporated into Algorithm
1
to further enhance precision. For instance, to improve the accuracy of
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}(y)
, we may want to ensure that the second-order derivative of
\hat{Q}_{1}(z)
matches those of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(-\lambda\hat{\varphi}(z))
. Since the derivatives are also linear in the weights
w_{k}
, it is straightforward to regularize the problem and add such constraints. Our numerical studies suggest that when
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
is relatively small (e.g.,
\hat{\gamma}_{2}\leq 1
), incorporating constraints on the second-order derivative of
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(-\lambda\hat{\varphi}(z))
can slightly improve the estimation accuracy of
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}
. However, when
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
is relatively large (e.g.,
\hat{\gamma}_{2}\geq 5
), such constraints tend to reduce estimation precision. This is mainly because the accuracy of
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime\prime}(z)
, which serves as the estimator of
\varphi^{\prime\prime}(z)
, diminishes when
p
becomes much larger than
n_{2}
.

Algorithm
1
is inspired by
El Karoui (2008)
and
Ledoit and Wolf (2012)
, but it diverges in the choice of basis functions and the design of the loss function. While
El Karoui (2008)
and
Ledoit and Wolf (2012)
prioritize achieving smoothness in the estimator of
F^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
by employing a combination of smooth basis functions and point masses, our focus is fundamentally different. In the context of estimating
s(x)
, the smoothness of
F^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
is not a primary concern.
Consequently, we adopt a simpler approach by approximating
F^{\Sigma_{\infty}}
solely using a mixture of point masses. Our numerical experiments indicate that this method performs comparably to, if not better than, the smooth basis approach, provided the grid of
\sigma_{k}
points is sufficiently dense. This finding suggests that the additional complexity introduced by smooth basis functions offers no substantial benefit in this specific setting. As a result, we focus exclusively on point-mass mixture models in our estimation procedure. In addition, the loss functions used by
El Karoui (2008)
and
Ledoit and Wolf (2012)
control only the discrepancy between
\hat{Q}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
. In contrast, our method explicitly penalizes the discrepancy between
\hat{Q}_{2}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, as accurate estimation of
\mathcal{H}_{2}
is of direct interest.

Implementation details for Algorithm
1
. The recommended choice of the grid
\{\sigma_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
is equally spaced on
[\tilde{\ell}_{n_{2}},\tilde{\ell}_{1}]
, with
K\approx 500
. The choice of
K
strikes a balance between computational efficiency and estimation accuracy.
The recommended choice of
\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{I}
is such that
\Re(\hat{\varphi}(z_{i}))
are evenly spaced on
[\hat{\varphi}(1.05\tilde{\ell}_{1}),\hat{\varphi}(-\lambda)]
, and
\Im(\hat{\varphi}(z_{i}))=10^{-2}\times\tilde{\ell}_{1}^{-1}
. We can first select
\hat{\varphi}(z_{i})
and numerically find the corresponding
z_{i}
using optimization methods like Newton-Raphson. While more points improve accuracy, we recommend setting
I\approx 500
to balance computational efficiency and estimation precision.
Traditional linear programming solvers can be used for implementation. In our simulation, we utilize the
R
package
Rglpk
, which implements the GNU Linear Programming Kit. With the recommended settings, the problem can be efficiently handled on a typical PC (
\leq 10
seconds).

Implementation details for Algorithm
3
. The proposed ODE requires high accuracy on the initial condition
\hat{s}(0)
. We propose using Newton-Raphson method to find
\hat{s}(0)
, starting from the initial point
1/(\lambda\hat{\gamma}_{2}\hat{\varphi}(-\lambda))-1/\hat{\gamma}_{2}
. The ODE can then be solved by traditional numerical methods such as the fourth-order Runge-Kutta method (RK4). In our simulation studies, the
R
package
deSolve
is used for implementation.

S.3
Effect of the regularization parameter: additional details

First, we give the explicit expressions for
\Theta_{H}
and
\Theta_{2p}(\infty)
involved in the SNR of the tests in the two limiting regimes when
\lambda\to 0
and
\lambda\to\infty
. Let
\operatorname{MP}_{p}(\tau)
be the Marčenko-Pastur law with parameter
\breve{\gamma}_{2}=\hat{\gamma}_{2}\wedge\hat{\gamma}_{2}^{-1}
, that is

d\operatorname{MP}_{p}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\pi\breve{\gamma}_{2}}\frac{\sqrt{[b_{+}-\tau][\tau-b_{-}]}}{\tau}d\tau,\quad\tau\in[b_{-},b_{+}],

where
b_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{\breve{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}
. Denote

s_{p0}(x)=\int\frac{d\operatorname{MP}_{p}(\tau)}{\tau-x},\quad\text{ for }~~x\in[0,b_{-}).

An explicit form of
s_{p0}(x)
is available; see, for example,
Bai and Silverstein (2010)
. Further, call

\breve{p}=p\wedge n_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\breve{n}_{1}=n_{1}\wedge(n_{1}+n_{2}-p).

Define
\beta=\beta_{p0}
to be the solution in
(0,b_{-})
to

\beta^{2}s^{\prime}_{p0}(\beta)=\frac{\breve{n}_{1}}{\breve{p}}.

Then,

\Theta_{H}=\left[\frac{p^{2}\breve{p}}{2\breve{n}_{1}^{3}}s^{\prime\prime}_{p0}(\beta)+\frac{p^{2}}{\breve{n}_{1}^{2}\beta^{3}}\right]^{1/3}.

It can be verified that
\Theta_{H}=p^{2/3}\breve{n}_{1}^{-2/3}\sigma_{p}^{-1}
, where
\sigma_{p}
is the quantity defined in (2.7) of
Han et al. (2016)
.

To obtain a bound on
\Theta_{H}^{-1}
, notice that

\Theta_{H}\geq\frac{p^{2/3}}{(n_{1}\wedge(n_{1}+n_{2}-p))^{2/3}}\frac{1}{\beta}\geq\frac{p^{2/3}}{n_{1}^{2/3}}\frac{1}{b_{-}}=\gamma_{1}^{2/3}(1-\sqrt{\breve{\gamma}_{2}})^{-2}.

Therefore,

\Theta_{H}^{-1}|\gamma_{2}-1|^{-1}\leq\gamma_{1}^{-2/3}(1-\sqrt{\breve{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}|1-\gamma_{2}|^{-1}.

If
\gamma_{2}<1
,

\Theta_{H}^{-1}|\gamma_{2}-1|^{-1}\leq\gamma_{1}^{-2/3}\frac{(1-\sqrt{\gamma_{2}})^{2}}{1-\gamma_{2}}=\gamma_{1}^{-2/3}\frac{1-\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}}{1+\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}}\sim\frac{1}{2\gamma_{1}^{2/3}}(1-\sqrt{\gamma_{2}}),\quad\text{if }\gamma_{2}\sim 1-.

If
\gamma_{2}>1
,

\Theta_{H}^{-1}|\gamma_{2}-1|^{-1}\leq\gamma_{1}^{-2/3}\gamma_{2}^{-1}\frac{(1-\sqrt{\breve{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}}{1-\breve{\gamma}_{2}}\sim\frac{1}{2\gamma_{1}^{2/3}}(1-\sqrt{\breve{\gamma}_{2}}),\quad\text{ if }\gamma_{2}\sim 1+.

Next, we consider the limiting case when
\lambda\to\infty
. The definition of
\Theta_{2p}(\infty)
is given as follows. Let

s_{p\infty}(x)=\int\frac{dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{1/\tau-x},\quad\text{for }~~x\in[0,1/\sigma_{1p}).

Define
\beta=\beta_{p\infty}
to be the solution in
(0,1/\sigma_{1p})
to

\beta^{2}s^{\prime}_{p\infty}(\beta)=\frac{n_{1}}{p}.

Then,

\Theta_{2p}(\infty)=\left[\frac{(p/n_{1})^{3}}{2}s^{\prime\prime}_{p\infty}(\beta)+\frac{(p/n_{1})^{2}}{\beta^{3}}\right]^{-1/3}.

Indeed,
\Theta_{2p}(\infty)=\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda\Theta_{2p}(\lambda)
. Also, it can be verified that
\Theta_{2p}(\infty)=(n_{1}/p)^{2/3}\gamma_{0}
, where
\gamma_{0}
is the quantity defined in (2.17) of
Lee and Schnelli (2016)
.

When
\gamma_{2}<1
, we have that
\ell_{H}=\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{0})
. Therefore, the limit of
p^{-s}\ell_{H}
follows directly from that of
p^{-s}\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})
as in Lemma
4.2
when we take
\lambda\to 0
. In particular,

\lambda\varphi_{p}(-\lambda)\to(1-\gamma_{2}),\quad\text{if }\gamma_{2}<1.

When
\gamma_{2}>1
,
\ell_{H}
is characterized as follows. Set
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}=\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}\mathbf{W}_{1}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}
and
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}=\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}\mathbf{W}_{2}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}
. Note that
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
is of rank
n_{2}
with probability 1. We denote the eigen-decomposition of
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
as

\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}=[Q_{1},Q_{2}]\begin{pmatrix}D~&~0\\
0~&~0\end{pmatrix}[Q_{1},Q_{2}]^{T}.

Here,
D
is
n_{2}\times n_{2}
and
Q_{1}
is
p\times n_{2}
. Then, following the analysis in (5.4)–(5.9) of
Han et al. (2016)
,
\ell_{H}
is the largest root to

\det\left(-\ell D+Q_{1}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}[I_{p}-\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}Q_{2}(Q_{2}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}Q_{2})^{-1}Q_{2}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}]\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}Q_{1}\right)=0.

Equivalently,
\ell_{H}
is the largest eigenvalue of

D^{-1}Q_{1}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}[I_{p}-\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}Q_{2}(Q_{2}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}Q_{2})^{-1}Q_{2}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}]\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{1/2}Q_{1}.

Notice that the matrix in the square bracket above is a projection matrix. We then have that
\ell_{H}
is bounded by

\ell_{H}\leq\text{the largest eigenvalue of }D^{-1}Q_{1}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}Q_{1},\text{or equivalently, that of }Q_{1}D^{-1}Q_{1}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}.

Since
Q_{1}D^{-1}Q_{1}^{T}
is the Moore-Penrose inverse of
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}
, denoted by
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}^{+}
, we have that

\ell_{H}\leq\ell_{\max}(\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}^{+}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}).

Under
PA
,
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}
is decomposed into

\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{(0)}+p^{s-1}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}\nu\nu^{T}D^{1/2}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}+n_{1}^{-1}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{p}BXP_{1}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}+n_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}P_{1}X^{T}B^{T}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{p}.

Here,
\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1}^{(0)}=n_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}P_{1}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}
. Following a well-known result in RMT, the largest (smallest) singular value of
n_{1}^{-1/2}\mathbf{{Z}}P_{2}
is bounded away from infinity (zero). We conclude that

\displaystyle p^{-s}\ell_{\max}(\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}^{+}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{1})

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\nu^{T}D^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1/2}_{p}\hat{\mathbf{W}}_{2}^{+}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}D^{1/2}\nu+o_{P}(1)

\displaystyle=p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(\hat{\mathbf{W}}^{+}_{2}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}D\Sigma^{-1/2}_{p})+o_{P}(1).

Recently, the asymptotic properties of the Moore-Penrose inverse of a large-dimensional sample covariance matrix have been studied in
Bodnar and Parolya (2024)
. In particular, Theorem 2.1 of
Bodnar and Parolya (2024)
implies that

p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(\hat{\mathbf{W}}^{+}_{2}\Sigma_{p}^{-1/2}D\Sigma^{-1/2}_{p})=\gamma_{2}^{-1}(\gamma_{2}-1)^{-1}p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(D\Sigma_{p}^{-1})+o_{P}(1).

The bound on
\ell_{H}
when
\gamma_{2}>1
follows.
∎

The specifications in the numerical study presented in Section
4.4
are as follows:

(1)

We consider four spectral structures for
\Sigma_{p}
:

(i)

(Identity)
\Sigma_{p}=I_{p}
.

(ii)

(Poly-Decay) The eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{p}
are:
(1+j/p)^{-2}
when
j=1,2,\dots,p
.

(iii)

(AR-ACF) The entries of
\Sigma_{p}
are
0.3^{|i-j|}
,
1\leq i,j\leq p
.

(iv)

(Point-Mix) The eigenvalues are 1, 5, 15 of proportion
40\%
,
30\%
, and
30\%
.

Under all settings, the spectrum is further normalized so that
\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{p})=p
.

(2)

We consider two settings for
D
. Namely,
D=I_{p}
and
D=\Sigma_{p}
.

Figure
S.3.1
displays the SNR as
\gamma_{2}
increases when
\gamma_{1}=2
. Figure
S.3.2
displays
{\rm SNR}_{p}(\lambda)
as a function of
\lambda
when
\gamma_{1}=0.5
.

)

). Black dashed:

).

(independent of

and

).

S.4
Simulation study: additional details

S.4.1
Simulation settings and competing methods

The study considers the following configurations:

(i)

The entries of the error matrix
\mathbf{{Z}}
are drawn from normal, t-distributions with 4 degrees of freedom, or Poisson distributions. However, as the results are consistent across these settings, only those for normal errors are reported.

(ii)

With
n_{2}=500
, the dimension
p
is set to
150
,
250
,
450
or
1000
, corresponding to
\hat{\gamma}_{2}=0.3
,
0.5
,
0.9
, and
2
.

(iii)

Three cases of
n_{1}
are considered:
n_{1}\in\{50,100,250\}
.

(iv)

The eigenvalues
\tau_{j}
of
\Sigma_{p}
are set according to three models:

(a)

Identity
:
\Sigma_{p}=I_{p}
.

(b)

Poly-Decay
:
\tau_{j}=(1+j/p)^{-2}
,
j=1,\dots,p
, representing polynomial decay.

(c)

AR-ACF
:
\Sigma_{p}
is a Toeplitz matrix with
(i,j)
-th entry
0.3^{|i-j|}
, for
i,j=1,\dots,p
.

(d)

Factor
: For
j\geq 6
, the eigenvalues
\tau_{j}
match those from the
Poly-Decay
model. The first five eigenvalues are spikes:
\tau_{j}=(2.2-0.2j)\tau_{6}
, for
j=1,\dots,5
. This represents a setting in which Condition
C5
is violated.

All models are further normalized so that
\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{p})=p
. The eigenvector matrix of
\Sigma_{p}
is randomly generated from the Haar distribution on the group of orthogonal matrices.

(v)

X
is a
n_{1}\times(n_{1}+n_{2})
matrix whose entries are iid
N(0,1)
. The coefficient matrix
B
(
p\times n_{1}
) is assumed to be of low rank with its first column drawn from
N(0,\zeta^{2})
and all other columns set to zero. The parameter
\zeta
controls the signal strength. We test
H_{0}:B=0
against
H_{a}:B\neq 0
. That is,
C=I_{n_{1}}
.

Additionally, while the technical power analysis in Section
4
focuses on a rank-one probabilistic alternative model, it is also of interest to examine the numerical performance of the proposed methods when the hypothesized matrix
BC
has high rank, so that the signal is distributed across a collection of eigendirections. Empirical power results for additional settings in which
BC
has rank
(n_{1}/2)
are reported in Section
S.4.5
.

Across all simulation settings, the estimators
\hat{\Theta}_{1}(\lambda)
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}(\lambda)
are computed using Algorithms
1
,
2
, and
3
, implemented with the recommended configurations detailed in Section
S.2
.

All competing methods are summarized as follows. For the proposed method, we consider five choices of
\lambda
:

(1)

\lambda=0.5
;

(2)

\lambda=1
;

(3)

the Bayes selection when
D\propto I_{p}
, with
\mathfrak{X}
chosen as recommended in Section
5
;

(4)

the Bayes selection when
D\propto\Sigma_{p}
, with
\mathfrak{X}
chosen as recommended in Section
5
; and

(5)

the estimated minimax selection when the prior family is

\left\{D_{\theta}=\theta_{1}I_{p}+\theta_{2}\Sigma_{p}:\;\theta_{1}\geq 0,\;\theta_{2}\geq 0,\;\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}\,p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{W}_{2})=1\right\},

with
\mathfrak{X}
again taken as in Section
5
.

The proposed method is compared with representative approaches from the literature. As discussed in the Introduction, most related methods fall into three broad categories. Since extreme-type tests—such as those of
Cai et al. (2014)
and
Chang et al. (2017)
—are not readily applicable to general linear hypotheses, we restrict attention to quadratic-form and projection-based procedures.

Ridge-LRT
:

We consider the ridge-regularized likelihood ratio test of
Li et al. (2020b)
, referred to as Ridge-LRT. The tests employs the same ridge regularization but aggregate all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}
through a
\log(1+x)
transformation. Specifically, we consider the Bayesian choice of the regularization parameter with the true prior presented in Section 3 of
Li et al. (2020b)
.

Proj-LRT
:

To benchmark projection-based methods, we note that most existing projection tests assume
n_{1}\ll p
and are not directly applicable to the general linear hypothesis setting considered here. We therefore construct a baseline procedure, denoted Proj-LRT, by projecting the observations onto random subspaces of dimension
\min(n_{1},n_{2},p)/10
, and then applying the likelihood ratio test to the projected data. A composite statistic is formed by taking the supremum over 20 such projections, with projection matrices drawn according to the Haar measure.

Han et. al. (2016)
:

We also include the test proposed by
Han et al. (2016)
, treated as the case of the proposed method when
\lambda\to 0
.

S.4.5
Results under high-rank alternatives

In this section, we examine the performance of the proposed methods under a class of high-rank alternatives and compare them with likelihood-based approaches, namely Ridge-LRT and Proj-LRT.

Specifically, while all other model configurations remain the same as those in Section
S.4.1
, we consider the following specification of
B
under the alternative:

(vi)

X
is an
n_{1}\times(n_{1}+n_{2})
matrix whose entries are i.i.d.
N(0,1)
. The coefficient matrix
B
(
p\times n_{1}
) is assumed to have rank
(n_{1}/2)
, where its first
(n_{1}/2)
columns are drawn from
N(0,\zeta^{2}D)
and the remaining columns are set to zero. Here
D=\operatorname{diag}(d_{1}^{2},\dots,d_{p}^{2})
, where
d_{j}
are evenly spaced between
2
and
10
, and
D
is further normalized so that
\mathrm{tr}(D)=p
. The parameter
\zeta
controls the signal strength. We test
H_{0}:B=0
against
H_{a}:B\neq 0
, that is,
C=I_{n_{1}}
.

The empirical power of all competing methods under this configuration is shown in Figures
S.4.12
–
S.4.15
. The results indicate that, although less efficient than likelihood-based approaches as expected, the largest-root tests still retain considerable power under high-rank alternatives.

), the latter available only when

.

S.5
Basic Definitions and Additional Properties of the M-P Equations

In the remaining sections, we present the proofs of all technical results stated in the manuscript. We begin in this section by introducing the necessary notations and definitions. We then provide additional results concerning the properties of the Marčenko–Pastur equation (
2.1
) and equation (
2.3
). Throughout this section, we assume Conditions
C1
–
C6
.

S.5.1
Basic definitions

Throughout, we use
a
,
a^{\prime}
,
c
,
c^{\prime}
,
C
, and
C^{\prime}
to denote generic constants whose values may change from line to line. Unless stated otherwise, these constants do not depend on
p
. We use
E=E(z)
and
\eta=\eta(z)
to denote the real and imaginary parts of a complex number
z
, respectively. The argument
z
is frequently omitted from expressions when no confusion may arise.

Definition S.5.1
(Matrix norms).

Let
A=(a_{ij})
be a complex matrix. We define the following norms

\|A\|=\max_{\|x\|=1}\|x^{*}Ax\|,\quad\|A\|_{\infty}=\max_{i,j}|a_{ij}|,\quad\|A\|_{F}=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(AA^{*})},

where
A^{*}
is the conjugate transpose of a matrix
A
. If
A
is a vector, we write
|A|=\|A\|
for simplicity.

The following notion of a high-probability bound has been used in a number of works on random matrix theory. It provides a simple way of systematizing and making precise statements of the form “
\xi
is bounded with high probability by
\zeta
up to small powers of
n
”.

Definition S.5.2
(Stochastic domination).

(i)

Consider two families of nonnegative random variables

\xi=\left(\xi^{(n)}(u):m\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),\quad\zeta=\left(\zeta^{(n)}(u):n\in\mathbb{N},u\in U^{(n)}\right),

where
U^{(n)}
is a possibly
n
-dependent parameter set. We say that
\xi
is stochastically dominated by
\zeta
, uniformly in
u
, if for all (small)
\varepsilon>0
and (large)
D>0
we have

\sup_{u\in U^{(n)}}\mathbb{P}\left[\xi^{(n)}(u)>n^{\varepsilon}\zeta^{(n)}(u)\right]\leqslant n^{-D}

for large enough
n\geqslant n_{0}(\varepsilon,D)
. Throughout this paper the stochastic domination will always be uniform in all parameters (such as matrix indices, deterministic vectors, and spectral parameters
z
) that are not explicitly fixed. If
\xi
is stochastically dominated by
\zeta
, uniformly in
u
, we use the notation
\xi\prec\zeta
. Moreover, if for some complex family
\xi
we have
|\xi|\prec\zeta
we also write
\xi=O_{\prec}(\zeta)
.

(ii)

We extend the definition of
O_{\prec}(\cdot)
to matrices in the weak operator sense as follows. Let
A
be a family of complex square random matrices and
\zeta
a family of nonnegative random variables. Then we use
A=O_{\prec}(\zeta)
to mean
|\langle\mathbf{v},A\mathbf{w}\rangle|\prec\zeta|\mathbf{v}||\mathbf{w}|
uniformly for all deterministic vectors
\mathbf{v}
and
\mathbf{w}
.

(iii)

If there exists a positive constant
C
such that
\xi\leq C\zeta
, then we write
\xi\lesssim\zeta
. If further there exists a positive constant
C^{\prime}
such that
\zeta\leq C^{\prime}\xi
, then we write
\xi\asymp\zeta
.

Definition S.5.3
.

We say that an event
\Lambda
holds with high probability if for any large positive constant
D
, there exists
n_{0}(D)
such that

\mathbb{P}(\Lambda^{\complement})\leq n^{-D},~\mbox{ for any }n\geq n_{0}(D).

S.5.2
Additional properties of the Marčenko-Pastur Equation

We present additional properties of the solution to the Marčenko-Pastur Equation (
2.1
) evaluated at the probability measure
F
being the distribution of
1/X
when
X\sim\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
and
\gamma=\hat{\gamma}_{1}
. That is, we study the properties of
q(z)=q_{\lambda}(z)
being the unique solution in
\mathbb{C}^{+}
to

z=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau+q}.

(S.5.1)

Following foundational results in RMT,
q(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of a probability measure, denoted by
\mathcal{F}_{q}
. It is further known that
\mathcal{F}_{q}
is compactly supported in
(0,\infty)
. It is understood that
q(z)
and
\mathcal{F}_{q}
depend on
p
and
\lambda
.

Recall the definition of
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)
:
\mathbb{C}^{+}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
as the Stieltjes transform of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
(See the statement right after Eq. (
2.3
)). That is,

\phi_{p\lambda}(z)=\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau-z},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

For convenience, we extend the definition of
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)
to
z\in\mathbb{C}^{-}=\{E+\mathrm{i}\eta:\eta<0\}
by setting
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)=\overline{\phi_{p\lambda}(\overline{z})}
if
\eta<0
. By doing so, we can concisely write (
S.5.1
) as

z=-\frac{1}{q(z)}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\phi_{p\lambda}(-q(z)).

We assume that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. In the remainder of this section, the parameter
\lambda
is fixed. We suppress the explicit dependence of many quantities on
\lambda
and
p
for notational simplicity. Unless stated otherwise, quantities introduced in this section may depend on
p
and
\lambda
, even when this dependence is not made explicit.

The properties of the solution to (
2.1
) have been studied in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. The application of the main theorems in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
requires verification of their key structural assumptions. In particular, we need to verify that Definition 2.7 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
is satisfied at the rightmost support edge of the distribution
\mathcal{F}_{q}
.

Recall that
s(x)=s_{p\lambda}(x)
denotes the extension of
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)
to the real line. Define the function

f(q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}s(-q),\qquad q\in(-\rho,0).

In the technical proof, we shall also consider
f(q)
evaluated at complex-valued
q
.

Recall also the definitions of
\beta=\beta_{p\lambda}
and
\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{1p}(\lambda)
given in Theorem
2.2
. Then,
-\beta
is the unique critical point of
f
in
(-\rho,0)
, since

f^{\prime}(-\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}s^{\prime}(\beta)=0.

Moreover,

\Theta_{1}=f(-\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}s(\beta).

Following well-known results in RMT,
\Theta_{1}
is the rightmost support edge of
\mathcal{F}_{q}
; see e.g.
Silverstein and Choi (1995)
.

To verify Definition 2.7 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
at the edge
\Theta_{1}
, we only need to show that there exists a small constant
c

\inf_{\tau\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda})}|\beta-\tau|=\rho-\beta\geq c,

for all sufficiently large
p
.

When Part (a) of Definition
2.2
holds, the density of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
exhibits a square-root behavior near
\rho
. Namely, we can find constants
C_{1}>0
,
C_{2}>0
and
\epsilon>0
such that for all sufficiently large
p
,

C_{1}\sqrt{x-\rho}\leq f_{\mathcal{G}}(x)\leq C_{2}\sqrt{x-\rho},\qquad\text{for all }x\in(\rho,\rho+\epsilon).

The results are shown in Theorem 5.1 of
Li (2024)
.

On the other hand, when Part (b) of Definition
2.2
holds, Theorem 3.2 of
Li (2024)
implies that
\rho
is an isolated point mass of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
, satisfying

\mathcal{G}_{\lambda}(\{\rho\})\geq F^{\Sigma_{p}}(\{\sigma_{1p}\})-\frac{n_{2}}{p}.

Note that the right hand side is bounded away from
0
under our assumptions.

Under both cases, as
x\uparrow\rho
,

s^{\prime}(x)=\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau-x}\to\infty.

Indeed, it is straightforward to verify that for any large constant
C
, we can find a small constant
c>0
such that

s^{\prime}(x)\geq C,\text{ if }x\in(\rho-c,~\rho),

for all sufficiently large
p
. Therefore, for any fixed
{\gamma}
, we can find a small constant
c>0
such that,

x^{2}s^{\prime}(x)>1/{\gamma},~~\text{if }x\in(\rho-c,~\rho).

Combining these results, we conclude that when
F^{\Sigma_{p}}
is regular, there exists a constant
c>0
such that

\rho-\beta\geq c

for all sufficiently large
p
. Consequently, the requirement in Definition 2.7 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
is satisfied at the edge
\Theta_{1}
.

We next present a combination of the main results in
Silverstein and Choi (1995)
,
Bao et al. (2015)
, and
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, together with references therein. The proof is therefore omitted.

Lemma S.5.1
.

Fix any
\lambda>0
. The following results are known for
\mathcal{F}_{q}
and
q(z)
.

(1)

Fix any small constant
a>0
. Then, there exists a constant
C>0
, depending on
a
, such that

C^{-1}\leq|q(z)|\leq C,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Im q(z)\geq C^{-1}\eta,

for all
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
satisfying
a\leq|z|\leq a^{-1}
.

(2)

There exists sufficiently small constants
c>0
and
c^{\prime}>0
such that for all
z\in\{E+\mathrm{i}\eta~:~|E-\Theta_{1}|\leq c,~~0<\eta<c^{-1}\}

\inf_{\tau\in S_{\mathcal{G}}}|\tau+q(z)|\geq c^{\prime},

where
S_{\mathcal{G}}
is the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
. It indicates that the equation (
S.5.1
) is non-singular when
z
is near
\Theta_{1}
.

(3)

There exists an open ball of
\Theta_{1}
(in
\mathbb{C}
), say
\mathfrak{N}_{\Theta_{1}}
such that when
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathfrak{N}_{\Theta_{1}}\cap\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\Im q(z)\asymp\begin{cases}\displaystyle\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}&\mbox{if }E<\Theta_{1},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}&\mbox{ if }E>\Theta_{1},\end{cases}

where
\kappa=|E-\Theta_{1}|
.

Next, we are interested in the behavior of Eq. (
S.5.1
) under a small perturbation of
z
, provided that
z
is near
\Theta_{1}
. For this purpose, we consider the following domains of
z
. For the positive constants
a
and
a^{\prime}
, we define

D=D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})\coloneqq\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~|E-\Theta_{1}|\leq a,~n_{1}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\leq\eta\leq 1/a^{\prime}\}.

(S.5.2)

In the subsequent analysis, we shall always choose
a
and
a^{\prime}
to be such that Result (3) of Lemma
S.5.1
holds for all
z\in D
.

The following lemma indicates that if a small perturbation is added to
z
when
z\in{D}
, Eq. (
S.5.1
) is stable in the sense that
q(z)
will only have small changes. The results are a combination of Definition 5.4 and Definition A.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. Therefore, the proof is omitted.

Lemma S.5.2
(Strong stability of Eq. (
S.5.1
)).

Eq. (
S.5.1
) is strongly stable in the following sense. Suppose that
\delta:{D}\to(0,\infty)
satisfies
p^{-2}\leq\delta(z)\leq 1/\log(p)
for
z\in{D}
and that
\delta
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
p^{2}
. Suppose moreover that for each fixed
z\in{D}
, the function
\eta\to\delta(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)
is non-increasing for
\eta>0
. Suppose that
u:{D}\to\mathbb{C}^{+}
is the Stieltjes transform of a compactly supported probability measure. Let
z\in{D}
and suppose that

\left|\tilde{z}-z\right|\leq\delta(z),

where
\tilde{z}=-{1}/{u(z)}+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(-u(z))
. Note that
z=-{1}/{q(z)}+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(-q(z))
and
u(z)=q(\tilde{z})
.

If
\Im z<1
, suppose also that

|u-q|\leq\frac{C\delta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\sqrt{\delta}}

(S.5.3)

holds at
z+\mathrm{i}p^{-5}
. Here,
\kappa=|E-\Theta_{1}|
. Then, Eq. (
S.5.3
) holds at
z
.

It is worth mentioning that if for some
z_{0}\in{D}
with
\eta(z_{0})\geq 1
,
|\tilde{z_{0}}-z_{0}|\leq\delta(z_{0})
is satisfied, then Eq. (
S.5.3
) holds for all
z
in

\Big\{z\mid E(z)=E(z_{0}),~~\eta(z_{0})-\eta(z)\in p^{-5}\mathbb{N}\Big\}\cap{D},

by induction.

S.5.3
Additional properties of the generalized Marčenk-Pastur Equation (
2.3
)

In this subsection, we present additional properties of
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)
. The focus is on the boundedness of
\phi_{p\lambda}(z)
around
\rho_{p\lambda}
and the stability of (
2.3
) against a small perturbation of
z
. These results are analogous to those for
q(z)
in Section
S.5.2
.

We assume that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. In the remainder of this section, unless stated otherwise, quantities introduced in this section may depend on
p
,
z
, and
\lambda
, even when this dependence is not made explicit. We frequently omit the dependence on
p
,
\lambda
, and
z
in many quantities, whenever no confusion may arise.

For the readers’ convenience, recall that as in Section
2.1
,
\phi(z)
is the solution to Eq. (
2.3
) evaluated at
F=F^{\Sigma_{p}}
and
\gamma=\hat{\gamma}_{2}
, that is

\phi(z)=\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau\{[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(z)]^{-1}-z\}+\lambda}.

Recall that, as explained in Section
2.1
, the equation can be reformulated to

z=h(z)+\left[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau h(z)+\lambda}\right]^{-1},

where
h(z)=z-[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(z)]^{-1}
. We have the definition

x_{p}(h)=h+\left[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau h+\lambda}\right]^{-1},

as the extension to the real line. In the technical proof, we shall also
x_{p}
for
h
being complex.

Further, recall that
\rho
is defined to be the leftmost edge of the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
and
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
is the probability measure associated with the Stieltjes transform
\phi(z)
.

Our analysis heavily relies on the main results of the companion paper
Li (2024)
. Proof of results in the rest of this section, if not provided, can be found in
Li (2024)
.

Lemma S.5.3
(Properties of
\phi(z)
and
h(z)
: Case (a)).

Suppose that the condition in Part (a) of Definition
2.2
is satisfied. Then, we have the following results.

(1)

Denote the unique critical point of
x_{p}(h)
in
(-\infty,\lambda/\sigma_{1p})
to be
h_{0}
. Then,
\rho=x_{p}(h_{0})>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
is continuous at
\rho
.

(2)

The generalized M-P equations are non-singular near
z=\rho
in the following sense. We can find constants
c>0
and
C>0
such that when
|z-\rho|\leq c
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

\inf_{\tau\in\operatorname{supp}(F^{\Sigma_{p}})}\Big|-\tau h(z)+\lambda\Big|>C\quad\text{, }\quad\left|1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau h(z)+\lambda}\right|>C,

\text{ and }\inf_{\tau\in\operatorname{supp}(F^{\Sigma_{p}})}\Big|\tau\{[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(z)]^{-1}-z\}+\lambda\Big|>C.

Due to the smoothness of
h(z)
and
\phi(z)
, the results also hold if
\eta(z)=0
and
|z-\rho|\leq c
.

(3)

The following bounds on the derivatives of
x_{p}(h)
at
h_{0}
hold. We can find constants
C>0
and
c>0
such that

|h_{0}|\asymp 1,\quad|x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})|\asymp 1,\quad|x^{\prime\prime\prime}_{p}(h)|\leq C,\quad\text{for }|h-h_{0}|\leq c.

(4)

There exists constants
c
,
C
and
C^{\prime}
such that for all
|z-\rho|\leq c
and
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
,

|\phi(z)|\leq C^{\prime},\quad\Im\phi(z)\geq C\eta,\quad|1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(z)|\geq C.

The following results are shown using Part (3) of Lemma
S.5.3
.

Lemma S.5.4
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
S.5.3
, suppose that we fix
c<1
to be sufficiently small. Then, if
z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
is such that
|z-\rho|\leq c
, we have

\Im h(z)\asymp\begin{cases}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}&\mbox{if }E>\rho,\\[5.0pt]
\dfrac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}&\mbox{ if }E<\rho;\end{cases}

\Im\phi(z)\asymp\begin{cases}\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}&\mbox{if }E>\rho,\\[5.0pt]
\dfrac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}&\mbox{ if }E<\rho;\end{cases}

where
\kappa=|E-\rho|
.

Choose
c
to be sufficiently small. Then, by Lemma
S.5.3
,

z-\rho=x_{p}(h(z))-x_{p}(h_{0})=\frac{x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})}{2}(h(z)-h_{0})^{2}+O(|h(z)-h_{0}|^{3}),\quad\text{for }\quad|z-\rho|\leq c.

It follows then, for
|E-h_{0}|\leq c
,

E-\rho=\frac{x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})}{2}(h(E)-h_{0})^{2}\big(1+O(|h(E)-h_{0}|)\big).

First, because
\rho
is the leftmost edge point of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
,
\Im\phi(E)=0
as
\phi(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
. It immediately follows that
\Im(h(E))=0
for
E<\rho
, since
h(E)=E-[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(E)]^{-1}
. Secondly, if
E>\rho
, taking square root on both sides of the equation, we obtain

\Im h(E)\asymp\sqrt{E-\rho}
. Together, we conclude the square-root behavior

\Im h(E)\asymp\sqrt{|E-\rho|}\mathbb{I}(E>\rho).

Since
z=h+(1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi)^{-1}
, the square-root behavior also holds for
\phi(z)
as

\Im\phi(E)\asymp\sqrt{|E-\rho|}\mathbb{I}(E>\rho).

By the inversion formula of the Stieltjes transform, it follows that the density
f_{\mathcal{G}}
of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
has a square-root behavior near the edge
\rho
. Namely, we can find constants
C
,
C^{\prime}
, and
\epsilon
such that

C_{1}\sqrt{E-\rho}\leq f_{\mathcal{G}}(E)\leq C_{2}\sqrt{E-\rho},\quad\rho\leq E\leq\rho+\epsilon.

Then, the described pattern of
\Im\phi(z)
follows easily. Lastly, the pattern of
\Im h(z)
is deduced through the relationship
z=h+(1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi)^{-1}
.
∎

On the other hand, if Part (b) of Definition
2.2
holds, we have the following result that follows directly from Theorem 3.2 of
Li (2024)
.

Lemma S.5.5
(Properties of
\phi(z)
and
h(z)
: Case (b)).

Suppose that Part (b) of Definition
2.2
holds.

(1)

Then,
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
and
\rho
is an isolated point of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
with mass
F^{\Sigma_{p}}(\{\sigma_{1p}\})-1/\hat{\gamma}_{2}
. Therefore, as
z\to\rho
,
\Im\phi(z)
diverges and
h(z)\to\rho
.

(2)

Part (2) and (4) of Lemma
S.5.3
still hold if additionally
|z-\rho|>c^{\prime}
for some small constant
c^{\prime}>0
.

In the rest of this section, we shall focus on the case when
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
is continuous at
\rho
. For the positive constants
a
and
a^{\prime}
, we define the following domains. First, we define a domain around
\rho
with the imaginary part at least
n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}
as

Q=Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~|E|\leq a^{-1},\quad E\leq\rho+a,\quad n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\leq\eta\leq 1/a^{\prime}\}.

Secondly, we restrict the real part to be smaller than
\rho
and define

Q_{-}=Q_{-}(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2}):E\leq\rho\}.

Thirdly, we define a domain that is away from the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
as

Q_{\rm away}=Q_{\rm away}(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~a\leq\operatorname{dist}(z,\mathcal{I})\leq a^{-1},\eta\geq n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\},

where
\mathcal{I}=[\rho,~(1+\sqrt{\hat{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}+\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})]
. Here, we are using the fact that the rightmost edge of the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
is bounded by
(1+\sqrt{\hat{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}+\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})
. Indeed, recall that

\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma^{-1}_{p}.

It is well-known in RMT that the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}
converges almost surely to
(1+\sqrt{\hat{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}
under Condition
C2
. The largest eigenvalue of
\lambda\Sigma^{-1}_{p}
is bounded by
\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})<\infty
,

In the subsequent analysis, we shall always choose
a
and
a^{\prime}
to be such that Part (2) and Part (4) of Lemma
S.5.3
hold for
z\in Q
.

The following lemma is analogous to Lemma
S.5.2
. It indicates that if a small perturbation is added to
z
when
z\in Q
, Eq. (
2.3
) is stable in the sense that
\phi(z)
or
h(z)
will only have limited changes.

Lemma S.5.6
(Strong stability of Eq. (
2.3
)).

Suppose that
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. Eq. (
2.3
) is strongly stable in
Q
in the following sense. Suppose that
\delta:Q\to(0,\infty)
satisfies
p^{-2}\leq\delta(z)\leq 1/\log(p)
for
z\in Q
and that
\delta
is Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant
p^{2}
. Suppose moreover that for each fixed
z\in Q
, the function
\eta\to\delta(E+\mathrm{i}\eta)
is non-increasing for
\eta>0
.
Suppose that
v(z)=z-[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho(z)]^{-1}
, where
\varrho(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of a compactly supported probability measure. Let
z\in Q
and suppose that

\left|\tilde{z}-z\right|\leq\delta(z),

where

\tilde{z}=v(z)+\left[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau v(z)+\lambda}\right]^{-1}.

Note that
v(z)=h(\tilde{z})
.

If
\Im z<1
, suppose also that

|v-h|\leq\frac{C\delta}{\sqrt{|E-\rho|+\eta}+\sqrt{\delta}}

(S.5.4)

holds at
z+\mathrm{i}p^{-5}
. Then, Eq. (
S.5.4
) holds at
z
.

We postpone the proof of Lemma
S.5.6
to the end of this section.

Moreover, when
z
is away from the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
. The bound can be improved. The following lemma holds for both the case
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
and
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
.

Lemma S.5.7
.

Fix any
\lambda>0
. Eq. (
2.3
) is strongly stable in
Q_{away}
in the following sense. Suppose that
\delta:Q_{away}\to(0,\infty)
satisfies
\delta(z)\leq 1/\log(p)
for
z\in Q_{\rm away}
. Suppose that
v(z)=z-[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho(z)]^{-1}
where
\varrho(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of a compactly supported probability measure. Let
z\in Q_{away}
and suppose that

\left|\tilde{z}-z\right|\leq\delta(z),

where

\tilde{z}=v(z)+\left[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau v(z)+\lambda}\right]^{-1}.

Then,

|v(z)-h(z)|\leq C\delta(z),

for any
z\in Q_{\rm away}
.

Corollary S.5.1
.

It is worth mentioning that if for some
z_{0}\in{Q}
with
\eta(z_{0})\geq 1
,
|\tilde{z_{0}}-z_{0}|\leq\delta(z_{0})
is satisfied, then following immediately by induction, Eq. (
S.5.4
) holds for all
z
in

\Big\{z\mid E(z)=E(z_{0}),~~\eta(z_{0})-\eta(z)\in p^{-5}\mathbb{N}\Big\}\cap{Q}.

Corollary S.5.2
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
S.5.6
, if Eq. (
S.5.4
) holds, then we also have

|\varrho-\phi|\leq\frac{C\delta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\sqrt{\delta}}.

It is obvious since

v-h=\frac{\hat{\gamma}_{2}(\phi-\varrho)}{(1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho)(1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi)}

and
|1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\phi(z)|\asymp 1
,
|1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho(z)|\asymp 1
when
z\in Q
.

Corollary S.5.3
.

Using Lemma
S.5.6
, we obtain the following result. For
z\in Q
, suppose that
\varrho(z)
satisfies

\varrho(z)=\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau\{[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho(z)+\delta_{2}(z)]^{-1}-z\}+\lambda}+\delta_{1}(z).

Here,
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
are such that
|\delta_{1}|
and
|\delta_{2}|
satisfy the conditions on
\delta
in Lemma
S.5.6
.
If
\Im z<1
, suppose also that

|\varrho-\phi|\leq\frac{C|\delta_{1}|+C|\delta_{2}|}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\sqrt{|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|}}

(S.5.5)

holds at
z+\mathrm{i}p^{-5}
. Then, Eq. (
S.5.5
) holds at
z
.

Lemma
S.5.6
can be proved by arguments very similar to those used in the proof of Lemma
S.5.2
in Section A.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. For this reason, we present only the main ideas, omitting many technical details.

Suppose first that
\Im z\geq a^{\prime}
for some constant
a^{\prime}>0
. Then, from the assumption
\delta(z)\leq 1/\log(p)
, we get
\Im\tilde{z}\geq a^{\prime}/2
and therefore, due to the uniqueness of the solution
\phi(z)
and
h(z)
to the generalized M-P equation, we find
v(z)=h(\tilde{z})
. Hence,

|v(z)-h(z)|=|h(\tilde{z})-h(z)|\leq\int_{z}^{\tilde{z}}|h^{\prime}(\zeta)||d\zeta|\leq C\delta(z).

Here, in the last step, we are using the fact that
h^{\prime}(\zeta)
is bounded when
\zeta
is away from the real line. It yields the bound

|v-h|\leq C\delta\leq\frac{C\delta}{\sqrt{|E-\rho|+\eta}+\sqrt{\delta}}

at
z
such that
\Im z\geq a^{\prime}
.

What remains is therefore the case
|z-\rho|\leq 2a^{\prime}
, which we assume for the rest of the proof. By Taylor’s expansion, we can find some
v_{0}
on the line segment between
v
and
h
that

x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(v_{0})(v-h)^{2}+x^{\prime}_{p}(h)(v-h)=\tilde{z}-z.

Suppose that

|v-h|\leq(\log p)^{-1/3}.

The condition will be dealt with later. Then, we claim that for small enough
a^{\prime}
, we have

|x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(v_{0})|\asymp 1\qquad|x^{\prime}_{p}(h)|\asymp\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}.

The claim is proved using arguments similar to those in the proof of (A.16) in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. Intuitively, since
v_{0}
is close to
h_{0}
and
|x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})|\asymp 1
, we have
|x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(v_{0})|\asymp 1
since the function is smooth. Secondly,

x^{\prime}_{p}(h)=\int_{h_{0}}^{h}x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(\zeta)d\zeta=\int_{h_{0}}^{h}x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})+O(|h-h_{0}|)d\zeta=(h-h_{0})x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(h_{0})+O(|h-h_{0}|^{2}).

Therefore,

|x^{\prime}_{p}(h)|\asymp|h-h_{0}|\asymp\sqrt{|z-\rho|}\asymp\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}.

All together, we write

x^{\prime\prime}_{p}(v_{0})(v-h)^{2}+x^{\prime}_{p}(h)(v-h)=O(\delta).

The remainder of the proof follows exactly the argument in the proof of Lemma 4.5 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
. In particular, we explicitly solve the quadratic equation for
(v-h)
and select the appropriate solution via a continuity argument. This selection relies on the bound

|v-h|\leq\frac{C\delta}{\sqrt{|E-\rho|+\eta}+\sqrt{\delta}},

which is assumed to hold at
z+\mathrm{i}p^{-5}
.

Lastly, the previous condition
|v-h|\leq(\log p)^{-1/3}
follows by continuity from the corresponding estimate at the neighboring point
z+\mathrm{i}p^{-5}
. We refer the reader to Section A.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
and Lemma 4.5 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
for full details.

∎

When
z\in Q_{\rm away}
,
|h^{\prime}(\tilde{z})|
is bounded. Therefore,

|v(z)-h(z)|=|h(\tilde{z})-h(z)|\leq\int_{z}^{\tilde{z}}|h(\zeta)||d\zeta|\leq C\delta(z).

∎

The result follows from Lemma
S.5.6
and Corollary
S.5.2
because the specified
\varrho
is such that

\left|\tilde{v}(z)+\left[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-\tau\tilde{v}(z)+\lambda}\right]^{-1}-z\right|\leq C(|\delta|_{1}+|\delta|_{2}),

where
\tilde{v}(z)=z-[1+\hat{\gamma}_{2}\varrho(z)]^{-1}
.
∎

S.6
Local law and edge rigidity for
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}

Recall that

\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}.

In this section, we establish a local law for the resolvent of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
and prove eigenvalue rigidity for its smallest eigenvalue.

Although all results remain valid for general distributions satisfying the moment conditions in Condition
C2
, we focus primarily on the Gaussian setting, since the extension to non-Gaussian distributions is not required for the proof of Theorem
2.2
. Nevertheless, such an extension, while technically more involved, can be carried out by following the arguments in Sections 7–9 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
in a largely verbatim manner. We discuss this extension in Theorem
S.6.4
.

First of all, when
\mathbf{{Z}}
is normally distributed,
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
has the same distribution as

\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}\overset{\mathrm{def}}{=\joinrel=}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1},

where
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}
is
p\times n_{2}
whose entries are iid
\mathcal{N}(0,1/n_{2})
. We focus on this representation in the following analysis.

Our study is divided into two regimes: (i)
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
and (ii)
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. We focus primarily on the first regime, where the analysis is more involved. The second regime is comparatively straightforward and is addressed in Section
S.6.5
.

Throughout the analysis, we consider the stochastic domination (Definition
S.5.2
) in the asymptotic regime
n_{2},p\to\infty
as in Condition
C1
, with
\lambda>0
fixed. Accordingly, we suppress the explicit dependence of quantities on
\lambda
, while retaining their dependence on
p
to avoid potential confusion.

We first introduce a fundamental control parameter as

\Psi(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\Im\phi_{p}(z)}{n_{2}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}.

Call the resolvent of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}

R(z)=\left(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}-zI_{p}\right)^{-1},\quad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

Recall the following domains defined in Section
S.5.3

\displaystyle Q=Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~|E|\leq a^{-1},\quad E\leq\rho+a,\quad n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\leq\eta\leq 1/a^{\prime}\},

\displaystyle Q_{-}=Q_{-}(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2}):E\leq\rho\},

\displaystyle Q_{\rm away}=Q_{\rm away}(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~a\leq\operatorname{dist}(z,\mathcal{I})\leq a^{-1},\eta\geq n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\}.

Define the
(p+n_{2})\times(p+n_{2})
linearizing block matrix

\mathbf{J}(z)=\mathbf{K}^{-1}(z),\quad\mathbf{K}(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI_{p}&\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\\
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}&-I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

(S.6.1)

The construction of a linearization matrix is a commonly used technique in RMT when study the local laws of various random matrices. Indeed,
\mathbf{J}
contains the resolvent
R(z)
as one of its blocks (see Lemma
S.6.1
). Unlike
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
directly,
\mathbf{K}(z)
is linear in
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}
. The dependence of
\mathbf{K}(z)
on each entry of
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}
is much easier to deal with.

Call

\Omega(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-h_{p}(z)I_{p}\right)^{-1}&0\\
0&(z-h_{p}(z))I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

Theorem S.6.1
(Strong local law).

Suppose that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. Further suppose that the observations are Gaussian and
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. For arbitrary
a^{\prime}>0
, there exists constants
a>0
such that the following local laws hold.

(i)

The entrywise local law in
Q
holds as

\mathbf{J}(z)-\Omega(z)=O_{\prec}(\Psi(z)),\quad z\in Q.

(ii)

The averaged local law in
Q
holds as

\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-\phi_{p}(z)=O_{\prec}\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}\right),\quad z\in Q,

where
\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)=p^{-1}\mathrm{tr}R(z)
.

(iii)

The averaged local law in
Q_{-}
holds as

\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-\phi_{p}(z)=O_{\prec}\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)}\right),

where
\kappa=|E-\rho_{p}|
.

(iv)

The averaged local law in
Q_{\rm away}
holds as

\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-\phi_{p}(z)=O_{\prec}\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}}\right).

Once Theorem
S.6.1
is ready, it is not hard to deduce the following rigidity of the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
.

Theorem S.6.2
(Rigidity of the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
).

Under the conditions of Theorem
S.6.1
, for any fixed
\epsilon\in(0,2/3)
, with high probability, there is no eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
in
(-\infty,\rho_{p}-n_{2}^{-2/3+\epsilon})
.

The following theorem shows the convergence of
linear spectral statistics
of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
.

Theorem S.6.3
.

Suppose the conditions of Theorem
S.6.1
hold. Consider the interval

\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}=[\rho_{p}-\epsilon,~(1+\sqrt{p/n_{2}})^{2}+\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})+\epsilon],

where
\epsilon
is any fixed positive constant.

(i)

Then, with high probability, all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
are contained in
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
.

(ii)

Let
f
be any function analytic on
\mathcal{I}(\infty)
with
\mathcal{I}(\infty)
being any open interval such that
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}\subset\mathcal{I}(\infty)
for all sufficiently large
p
. The existence of
\mathcal{I}(\infty)
is straightforward under the conditions.
Denote by
\mathfrak{M}
the event that all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
are contained in
\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}
. We have

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{M})\left|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}f(\ell_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}))-\int f(\tau)d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)\right|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

For the proof of Theorem
2.2
, it suffices to establish the local laws and rigidity results under Gaussianity. Nevertheless, these results can be extended to the non-Gaussian setting.

Theorem S.6.4
.

Theorems
S.6.1
,
S.6.2
, and
S.6.3
remain valid when the Gaussianity assumption is removed.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
S.6.1
, Theorem
S.6.2
, and Theorem
S.6.3
. We also discuss the proof of Theorem
S.6.4
.

S.6.1
Proof of Theorem
S.6.1

The analysis proceeds in two steps. First, we assume that
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal, with diagonal entries
\sigma_{1p}\geq\sigma_{2p}\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_{pp}
. We then extend the results to a general covariance matrix
\Sigma_{p}
.

Our analysis follows the framework developed in Sections 4 and 5 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. Accordingly, we omit many technical details and refer the reader to the corresponding arguments therein. Throughout, we frequently suppress the argument
z
from our notation.

S.6.1.1
Basic tools

We make the following notation. We use
A_{st}
to denote the
(s,t)
-th element of a matrix
A
. The four blocks of
\mathbf{J}(z)
are called
\mathbf{J}_{(11)}
,
\mathbf{J}_{(12)}
,
\mathbf{J}_{(21)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{(22)}
. For a set of indices, say
S\subset\{1,2,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
, we define the minors
\mathbf{K}^{(S)}=(\mathbf{K}_{st},s,t\notin S)
. That is,
\mathbf{K}
but with the rows and columns whose indices are specified in
S
deleted. We also write
\mathbf{J}^{(S)}=(\mathbf{K}^{(S)})^{-1}
. The block formed by the first
p
rows and columns of
\mathbf{J}^{(S)}
is called
\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(S)}
. The block formed by the last
n_{2}
rows and columns of
\mathbf{J}^{(S)}
is called
\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(S)}
. Similar for
\mathbf{J}_{(12)}^{(S)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{(21)}^{(S)}
. Similar notation is made for the matrix
\Omega
.

For
u=1,2,\dots,p
, call the
u
-th row of
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}
to be
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}
. For
i=p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}
, we use
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}
to denote the
(i-p)
-th column of
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}
. Moreover, if
u\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, let
e_{u}
denote the standard unit vector of dimension
p
in the coordinate direction
u
. If
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
, let
e_{i}
denote the standard unit vector of dimension
n_{2}
in the coordinate direction
(i-p)
.

The following lemma is a variant of Lemma 4.4 in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. These results follow directly from the Schur complement formula and resolvent identities that have been previously derived in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
and the references therein. The proof is therefore omitted.

Lemma S.6.1
(Resolvent Identities).

Suppose that
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal with diagonal elements
\sigma_{1p},\dots,\sigma_{pp}
.

(i)

We have

\mathbf{J}=\left(\begin{matrix}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}&&&&\mathbf{J}_{(12)}\\
\mathbf{J}_{(21)}&&&&\mathbf{J}_{(22)}\end{matrix}\right)=\left(\begin{matrix}R&&&&R\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\\
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}R&&&&\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}R\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}-I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

(ii)

For
u\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
, we have

\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{uu}}=\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(u)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}^{T}.

For
u\neq v\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
,

\mathbf{J}_{uv}=-\mathbf{J}_{uu}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(u)}e_{v}=-\mathbf{J}_{vv}e_{u}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(v)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{v\cdot}^{T}=\mathbf{J}_{uu}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(u)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(uv)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{v\cdot}^{T}.

(iii)

For
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
, we have

\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{ii}}=-1-\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(i)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}.

For
i\neq j\in\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
, we have

\mathbf{J}_{ij}=-\mathbf{J}_{ii}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(i)}e_{j}=-\mathbf{J}_{jj}e_{i}^{T}\mathbf{J}^{(j)}_{(11)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot j}=\mathbf{J}_{ii}\mathbf{J}_{jj}^{(i)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}_{\cdot i}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(ij)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot j}.

(iv)

For
u\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
and
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
,

\mathbf{J}_{ui}=-\mathbf{J}_{ii}e_{u}^{T}\mathbf{J}^{(i)}_{(11)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}.

(v)

If
i,j,k\notin S
and
i,j\neq k
,

\mathbf{J}_{ij}^{(S)}=\mathbf{J}_{ij}^{(S\cup\{k\})}+\frac{\mathbf{J}_{ik}^{(S)}\mathbf{J}_{kj}^{(S)}}{\mathbf{J}_{kk}^{(S)}}.

The following result is our fundamental tool for estimating entries of
\mathbf{J}
.

Lemma S.6.2
.

Fix
a>0
. Then, the following estimates hold for any
z\in Q
. We have

\|\mathbf{J}\|\leq\frac{C}{\eta^{3}},\quad\quad\quad\|\partial z\mathbf{J}\|\leq\frac{C}{\eta^{6}}.

Furthermore, let
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{2}}
. Then, we have the bounds

\displaystyle\sum_{u=1}^{p}|\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}e_{u}|^{2}=\frac{\Im\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}\mathbf{w}}{\eta},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}e_{i}|^{2}\leq\frac{C\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|}{\eta}\Im\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}\mathbf{v}+C\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v},

\displaystyle\sum_{u=1}^{p}|\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(21)}e_{u}|^{2}=\frac{\Im\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}\mathbf{v}}{\eta},

\displaystyle\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(12)}e_{i}|^{2}\leq C\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\sum_{u=1}^{p}|\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}e_{u}|^{2}.

The estimates remain true for
\mathbf{J}^{(S)}
instead of
\mathbf{J}
if
S\subset\{1,\dots,p\}
or
S\subset\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
.

First, consider the bound on the operator norm of
\mathbf{J}
.

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{J}\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\left(\begin{matrix}(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI_{p})^{-1}&0\\
0&I\end{matrix}\right)\right\|\left\|\left(\begin{matrix}I_{p}&(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI_{p})^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\\
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI_{p})^{-1/2}&-I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right)^{-1}\right\|

\displaystyle=\|J_{1}\|\|J_{2}\|,\mbox{ say}.

For
J_{1}
, we clearly have
\|J_{1}\|\leq C/(|E-\lambda/\sigma_{1p}|+\eta)\leq C/\eta
, for
z\in Q
. The eigenstructure of a matrix of the form
J_{2}
is analyzed in (4.12-4.15) of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. Based on their arguments,

\displaystyle\|J_{2}\|\leq C(1+\max_{u}\frac{|s_{u}|+1}{|s_{u}-1|})

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\max_{u}\frac{(C/\eta)\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|+1}{|s_{u}-1|})

\displaystyle\leq C+(C/\eta)\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\|\lambda\Sigma^{-1}_{p}-zI\|\|(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}-(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI))^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq C+\frac{C}{\eta^{2}},

where
s_{k}
’s are the eigenvalues of
(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI)^{-1/2}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-zI)^{-1/2}
. The bound on
\|\mathbf{J}\|
follows.

The bound on the operator norm of
\partial_{z}\mathbf{J}
follows from the fact that
\|\partial_{z}\mathbf{J}\|=\|\mathbf{J}(\partial_{z}\mathbf{K})\mathbf{J}\|\leq\frac{C}{\eta^{6}}
.

Next, using Result (i) of Lemma
S.6.1
,

\sum_{u=1}^{p}|\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}e_{u}|^{2}=\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}e_{u}e_{u}^{T}\overline{\mathbf{J}}_{(11)}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}\overline{\mathbf{J}}_{(11)}\mathbf{w}=\mathbf{w}^{T}\frac{\Im\mathbf{J}_{(11)}}{\eta}\mathbf{w}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}

\displaystyle|\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}e_{i}|^{2}=\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|\mathbf{v}^{T}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}R\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}e_{i}-\mathbf{v}^{T}e_{i}|^{2}\leq 2\mathbf{v}^{T}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\overline{\mathbf{J}}_{(11)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\mathbf{v}+2\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\mathbf{v}^{T}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}\overline{\mathbf{J}}_{(11)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\mathbf{v}+C\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v}\leq C\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\frac{\Im\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}\mathbf{v}}{\eta}+C\mathbf{v}^{T}\mathbf{v}.

The remaining two results on
\mathbf{J}_{(12)}
and
\mathbf{J}_{(21)}
can be proved similarly using Result (i) of Lemma
S.6.1
. We omit the details.
∎

Lemma S.6.3
.

Under
C1
,
\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\leq(1+\sqrt{p/n_{2}})^{2}+\epsilon
with high probability, for any fixed
\epsilon>0
.

The lemma follows from Theorem 2.10 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
.

S.6.1.2
Weak entrywise law

We first show a weak entrywise local law of
\mathbf{J}
in
Q
.

Proposition S.6.1
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
S.6.1
hold. Define

\Lambda=\max_{1\leq s,t\leq p+n_{2}}|(\mathbf{J}-\Omega)_{st}|\quad\mbox{and}\quad\Lambda_{o}=\max_{s\neq t}|(\mathbf{J}-\Omega)_{st}|.

Then,
\Lambda\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/4}
uniformly in
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
.

Define the averaged control parameter

\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}_{p}+\mathfrak{B}_{n},\quad\mathfrak{B}_{p}=\left|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{i=u}^{p}(\mathbf{J}_{uu}-\Omega_{uu})\right|,\quad\mathfrak{B}_{n}=\left|\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}(\mathbf{J}_{ii}-\Omega_{ii})\right|.

Note that
\mathfrak{B}_{p}=|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|
. We have the trivial bound
\mathfrak{B}\leq C\Lambda
.

For
s\in\{1,2,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
, we introduce the conditional expectation

\mathbb{E}_{s}[~\cdot~]=\mathbb{E}[~\cdot~\mid\mathbf{K}^{(s)}].

Using Lemma
S.6.1
, we get that, for
u\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,

\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{uu}}=\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathrm{tr}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(u)}-W_{u},\quad\quad W_{u}=(1-\mathbb{E}_{u})(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(u)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}^{T}).

(S.6.2)

For
i\in\{p+1,\cdots,p+n_{2}\}
,

\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{ii}}=-1-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\mathrm{tr}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(i)}-W_{i},\quad\quad W_{i}=(1-\mathbb{E}_{i})(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}^{T}\mathbf{J}_{(11)}^{(i)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{\cdot i}).

(S.6.3)

We define the
z
-dependent event
\Xi=\{\Lambda\leq 1/(\log n_{2})\}
and the control parameter

\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}=\sqrt{\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+\mathfrak{B}}{n_{2}\eta}}.

The following estimate is analogous to Lemma 5.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
.

Lemma S.6.4
.

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
S.6.1
hold. Then, uniformly for
s\in\{1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
and
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\Xi)(|W_{s}|+\Lambda_{o})\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)(|W_{s}|+\Lambda_{o})\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}.

The proof relies on the identities from Lemma
S.6.1
and large deviation estimates like Lemma 3.1 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
. The arguments in the proof are similar to those in Lemma 5.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
.

From Lemma
S.5.3
, when
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
,
|\Omega_{ss}|\asymp 1
. Therefore,

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\mathbf{J}_{ss}\asymp 1.

Using Result (v) of Lemma
S.6.1
and a simple induction argument, it is not hard to conclude that

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\mathbf{J}_{ss}^{(S)}\asymp 1,

for any
S\subset\{1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
and
s\notin S
, satisfying
|S|\leq C
.

Let us first estimate
\Lambda_{o}
. When
u\neq v\in\{1,\dots,p\}
, using Result (ii) of Lemma
S.6.1
and a large deviation estimation (Lemma 3.1 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
),

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)|\mathbf{J}_{uv}|\leq\mathbb{I}(\Xi)|\mathbf{J}_{uu}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(u)}|\left|\sum_{i,j=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\tilde{Z}_{ui}\tilde{Z}_{vj}\mathbf{J}^{(uv)}_{i,j}\right|\prec\mathbb{I}(\Xi)C\left(\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|\mathbf{J}_{ij}^{(uv)}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}.

(S.6.4)

The term in parentheses is

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|\mathbf{J}_{ij}^{(uv)}|^{2}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}}\sum_{i,j=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}|e_{i}^{T}\mathbf{J}^{(uv)}_{22}e_{j}|^{2}

\displaystyle\prec\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}\eta}\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\Im\mathbf{J}_{ii}^{(uv)}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}}

\displaystyle\prec\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}^{2}\eta}\|\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}\|\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\Im\mathbf{J}_{ii}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{\Lambda_{o}^{2}}{n_{2}\eta}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}}

\displaystyle\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+\mathfrak{B}+\Lambda_{o}^{2}}{n_{2}\eta}+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+\mathfrak{B}+\Lambda_{o}^{2}}{n_{2}\eta}.

Here, we are using Result (v) of Lemma
S.6.1
, Lemma
S.6.3
and Lemma
S.6.2
. The last step is due to the fact that
1\prec{\Im\phi_{p}}/\eta
.

A similar result for
\mathbf{J}_{ij}
,
\mathbf{J}_{ui}
, and
\mathbf{J}_{iu}
,
i\in\{p+1,p+n_{2}\}
and
u\in\{1,p\}
can be obtained using Result (iii) and (iv) of Lemma
S.6.1
and a large deviation estimation. We omit the details as the arguments are very similar.

All together, we conclude that

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\Lambda_{o}\prec\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\sqrt{\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+\mathfrak{B}+\Lambda_{o}^{2}}{n_{2}\eta}}.

Since
1/\sqrt{n_{2}\eta}\to 0
when
z\in Q
, it is easy to deduce that
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\Lambda_{0}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

An analogous argument for
|W_{s}|
(see e.g. Lemma 5.2 of
Erdős et al. (2013)
) completes the proof of the statement in Proposition
S.6.1
when
\Xi
holds.

As for the case when
\eta\geq 1
, we proceed similarly. For
\eta\geq 1
, we proceed as above to get
|W_{s}|\prec n_{2}^{-1/2}
, where we used that
\|\mathbf{J}\|\leq C/\eta\leq C
by Lemma
S.6.2
. Similarly, as in Eq. (
S.6.4
), we get

\mathbf{J}_{uv}\leq|\mathbf{J}_{uu}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(u)}|\left|\sum_{i,j=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\tilde{Z}_{ui}\tilde{Z}_{vj}\mathbf{J}^{(uv)}_{i,j}\right|\prec|\mathbf{J}_{uu}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(u)}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}},

where in the last step we used that
|\Im\mathbf{J}^{(uv)}_{ii}|\leq C
because
\|\mathbf{J}\|\leq C
. Moreover,
|\mathbf{J}_{uu}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(u)}|\leq C
. This completes the proof of Lemma
S.6.4
.
∎

In the following, we aim to show that
\hat{\phi}_{p}
satisfies a perturbed version of Eq. (
2.3
) when
\Xi
holds or when
\eta\geq 1
. From Eq. (
S.6.2
), Eq. (
S.6.3
), Lemma
S.6.4
, Result (v) of Lemma
S.6.1
, we get that uniformly for
u\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
i\in\{p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}\}
,
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
,

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{uu}}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\left(\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\mathbf{J}_{ii}-W_{u}+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})\right),

(S.6.5)

-\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{ii}}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\left(1+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbf{J}_{uu}+W_{i}+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})\right).

(S.6.6)

Invert Eq. (
S.6.6
), get the Taylor’s expansion of the right-hand side to the order of
W_{i}^{2}
, and average the equation over
i=p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}
. We obtain

-\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\mathbf{J}_{ii}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbf{J}_{uu}}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{1},

(S.6.7)

where the residual
\delta_{1}
is

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{1}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{n}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}O_{\prec}(|W_{i}|^{2})+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{n}+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}),

[W]_{n}=\frac{1}{(1+\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbf{J}_{uu})^{2}}\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}W_{i}.

Here, we are using Lemma
S.6.4
.

Similarly, from Eq. (
S.6.5
),

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\mathbf{J}_{uu}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\mathbf{J}_{ii}}+\delta_{2},

where

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{2}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{p}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}O_{\prec}(|W_{u}|^{2})+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{p}+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}),

[W]_{p}=\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\frac{-1}{(\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\mathbf{J}_{ii})^{2}}W_{u}.

All together, we obtain

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\hat{\phi}_{p}=\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\int\frac{dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\lambda/\tau-z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+(p/n_{2})\hat{\phi}_{p}}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{1}}+\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{2},

which is a perturbed version of Eq. (
2.3
). Note that Lemma
S.6.4
guarantees that
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{1}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
and
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{2}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
. This estimate is actually not tight. We shall later improve the estimate to
O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})
.

On the other hand, it is not hard to identify that the derivation actually only relies on the fact that
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)(|W|_{s}+\Lambda_{o})\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
from Lemma
S.6.4
. Since the same control holds when
\eta\geq 1
. We obtain that

\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)\hat{\phi}_{p}=\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)\int\frac{dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\lambda/\tau-z-\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+(p/n_{2})\hat{\phi}_{p}}+\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)\delta_{1}}+\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)\delta_{2},

and
\mathbb{I}(\eta\geq 1)\delta_{1}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
,
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\delta_{2}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

We apply Lemma
S.5.6
, Lemma
S.5.7
, and Corollary
S.5.3
to control the difference between
\hat{\phi}_{p}
and
\phi_{p}
when
\eta\geq 1
. In particular, we have the following results.

Lemma S.6.5
.

Suppose that the conditions of Theorem
S.6.2
hold. Then, we have
\Lambda\prec n_{2}^{-1/2}
uniformly in
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
satisfying
\eta\geq 1
.

Applying Lemma
S.6.4
,
\Lambda_{o}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}\prec n_{2}^{-1/2}
. Here, we are using the fact that
\Im\phi_{p}+\mathfrak{B}=O(1)
, when
\eta\geq 1
.
We only need to show that the diagonal elements are such that

\mathbf{J}_{uu}-\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-h_{p}}\prec n_{2}^{-1/2},\quad u=1,2,\dots,p,

-\mathbf{J}_{ii}-\frac{1}{1+p/n_{2}\phi_{p}}\prec n_{2}^{-1/2},\quad i=p+1,p+2,\dots,p+n_{2}.

Applying Lemma
S.5.7
or Corollary
S.5.3
, when
\eta\geq 1
,

|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|\leq{C(|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|)}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}\prec n_{2}^{-1/2}.

Then, by Eq. (
S.6.6
)

-\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{ii}}=1+\phi_{p}+(\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p})+W_{i}+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2})=1+\phi_{p}+O_{\prec}(n_{2}^{-1/2}).

Therefore,
-\mathbf{J}_{ii}-\frac{1}{1+\phi_{p}}\prec n_{2}^{-1/2}
. The estimate of
\mathbf{J}_{uu}
is obtained similarly.
∎

To show Proposition
S.6.1
, we apply the stochastic continuity argument, exactly as in Section 4 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
. The argument allows us to propagate the smallness of
\Lambda
from
\eta\geq 1
to the whole domain
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
. Note that in the domain,
\eta\geq n_{2}^{-1+a^{\prime}}
. We choose
\varepsilon<a^{\prime}/4
and an arbitrary
D>0
. Let

L(z)=\{z\}\cup\{s\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}~:~\Re s=\Re z,\quad\Im s\in[\Im z,1]\cap[n_{2}^{-5}\mathbb{N}]\}.

We introduce the random function

v(z):=\max_{s\in L(z)}\Lambda(s)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}

Our goal is to prove that with high probability, there is a gap in the range of
v
, i.e.

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\quad v(z)>n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)\leqslant N^{-D+5}

(S.6.8)

for all
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
and large enough
n_{2}
. This equation says that with high probability the range of
v
has a gap: it cannot take values in the interval
\left(n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2},n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right]
.

Since we are dealing with random variables, one has to keep track of the probabilities of exceptional events. To that end, we only work on a discrete set of values of
\eta
, which allows us to control the exceptional probabilities by a simple union bound.

Next, we prove Eq. (
S.6.8
). First, Lemma
S.6.4
yields
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)(|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|)\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}\leq(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2}
. Since
\left\{v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right\}\subset\Xi(z)\cap\Xi(s)
for all
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
and
s\in L(z)
, we find that for all
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
and
s\in L(z)
that

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},~~(|\delta_{1}(s)|+|\delta_{2}(s)|)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/2}>n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D}

for large enough
n_{2}
(independent of
z
and
w
). Using a union bound, we therefore get
