\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},~~\max_{s\in L(z)}(|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/2}>n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+5}

Next, applying Lemma
S.5.6
and Lemma
S.5.7
with the perturbation
n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}(n_{2}\Im s)^{-1/2}
, we get

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},~~\max_{s\in L(z)}\mathfrak{B}_{p}(s)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}>n_{2}^{\varepsilon/4}\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+5}.

Moreover, since
-\mathbf{J}_{ii}-(1+(p/n_{2})\phi_{p})^{-1}=O_{\prec}(\mathfrak{B}_{p})+O_{\prec}(\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}})
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},~~\max_{s\in L(z)}\mathfrak{B}(s)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}>2n_{2}^{\varepsilon/4}\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+5}.

Then, Eq. (
S.6.8
) follows from the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},v(s)\geq n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\Lambda(s)(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}\geq n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}\geq n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\mathfrak{B}(n\Im s)^{-1}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/2}\geq(1/2)n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right)+n^{-D+5}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\mathfrak{B}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}\geq n_{2}^{\varepsilon}(n_{2}\Im s)^{3/4}/2\right)+n^{-D+5}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\mathfrak{B}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/4}\geq n_{2}^{\varepsilon}(n_{2})^{3a^{\prime}/4}/2\right)+n^{-D+5}

\displaystyle\leq 2n_{2}^{-D+5}.

Here, we are using the fact that for all
s\in L(z)

\frac{\Im\phi_{p}(s)}{n_{2}\Im s}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/2}=\frac{\Im\phi_{p}(s)}{\sqrt{n_{2}\Im s}}\prec 1.

Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)\leqslant n_{2}^{\varepsilon},\max_{s\in L(z)}\Im\phi_{p}(s)(n\Im s)^{-1}(n_{2}\Im s)^{1/2}\geq(1/2)n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right)\leq n_{2}^{-D+5}.

We conclude the proof of Proposition
S.6.1
by combining Eq. (
S.6.8
) and Lemma (
S.6.5
) with a continuity argument. We choose a lattice
\Delta\subset Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
such that
|\Delta|\leqslant n_{2}^{10}
and for each
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
there exists a
s\in\Delta
satisfying
|z-s|\leqslant N^{-4}
. Then, Eq. (
S.6.8
) combined with a union bound yields

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists s\in\Delta:v(s)\in\left(n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2},n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right]\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+15}

From the definitions of
\Lambda
and
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
, we find that
v
is Lipschitz continuous, with Lipschitz constant
n_{2}^{2}
.

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}~:~v(z)\in\left(2n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2},n_{2}^{\varepsilon}\right]\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+15}

By Lemma
S.6.5
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(v(z)>n_{2}^{\varepsilon/2}\right)\leqslant n_{2}^{-D+15}

for any
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
with
\eta\geq 1
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}(\max_{z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}}v(z)>2n_{2}^{\epsilon/2})\leq 2n_{2}^{-D+15}.

It completes the proof of Proposition
S.6.1
.

S.6.1.3
The averaged local law

We use Proposition
S.6.1
and improved estimates for the averaged quantities
[W]_{p}
and
[W]_{n}
to deduce the averaged local laws in
Q
,
Q_{-}
, and
Q_{\rm away}
, as in Theorem
S.6.1
. Indeed, we shall show that

\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{p}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathbb{I}(\Xi)[W]_{n}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}.

If so, the estimate of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
is improved to
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)|\delta_{1}|\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}
and
\mathbb{I}(\Xi)|\delta_{2}|\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}
.

Lemma S.6.6
(Fluctuation averaging).

Suppose that the assumptions of Theorem
S.6.1
hold. Suppose that
\Upsilon
is a positive,
n_{2}
-dependent, deterministic function on
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
satisfying
n_{2}^{-1/2}\leqslant\Upsilon\leqslant n_{2}^{-c}
for some constant
c>0
. Suppose moreover that
\Lambda\prec n_{2}^{-c}
and
\Lambda_{o}\prec\Upsilon
on
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
. Then on
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
we have

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\frac{1}{(\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\mathbf{J}_{ii})^{2}}\left(1-\mathbb{E}_{u}\right)\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{uu}}=O_{\prec}\left(\Upsilon^{2}\right)

and

\frac{1}{n_{2}}\sum_{i=p+1}^{p+n_{2}}\left(1-\mathbb{E}_{i}\right)\frac{1}{\mathbf{J}_{ii}}=O_{\prec}\left(\Upsilon^{2}\right).

Th results are an extension of Lemma 5.6 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, Lemma 4.9 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
, Theorem 4.7 of
Erdős et al. (2013)
. They can be proved using exactly the same method. We therefore omit the details. The readers may refer to the proof of Theorem 4.7 of
Erdős et al. (2013)
for details.

Note that Proposition
S.6.1
implies that
1-\mathbb{I}(\Xi)\prec 0
, that is
\Xi
holds with high probability. In the following analysis, we shall always assume
\Xi
holds. Combining with
Lemma
S.6.4
, we have

\Lambda\prec\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{1/4}},\quad\Lambda_{o}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}},

on
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
. Therefore, the condition of Lemma
S.6.6
is satisfied and we obtain

|[W]_{p}|+|[W]_{n}|\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2},

on
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
. Recall that
\delta_{1}=[W]_{n}+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}_{\mathfrak{B}})
and
\delta_{2}=[W]_{p}+O_{\prec}(\Psi^{2}_{\mathfrak{B}})
. We immediately get

(|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|)\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2},

uniformly in
Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
.

The following lemma holds immediately from the definition of
\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}
.

Lemma S.6.7
.

Let
c>0
and suppose that we have
\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}
uniformly in
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
. Then, we have

|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}

uniformly in
z\in Q\cup Q_{\rm away}
.

We first consider the averaged local law in
Q
. Suppose
\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}
. Then, uniformly in
z\in Q
,

|\delta_{1}|+|\delta_{2}|\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}.

We invoke Lemma
S.5.6
to get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{p}=

\displaystyle|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|\prec\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}+\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}}}\leq\frac{\Im\phi_{p}}{n_{2}\eta}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}+\sqrt{\frac{(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}}

(S.6.9)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n_{2}\eta}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2-c/2}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2-c/2}.

In the fourth step, we are using the expression of
\Im\phi_{p}
as shown in Lemma
S.5.4
. As for
\mathfrak{B}_{n}
, plugging the estimate of
\hat{\phi}_{p}
back to Eq. (
S.6.7
), we also have

\mathfrak{B}_{n}\prec\mathfrak{B}_{p}+\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}^{2}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2-c/2}+\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2-c/2}.

All together, we have shown that

\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}\quad\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\quad\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/2-c/2}.

(S.6.10)

From Proposition
S.6.1
, we know that
\mathfrak{B}\leq C\Lambda\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/4}
. Therefore, for any
\varepsilon>0
, we iterate Eq. (
S.6.10
) finite number of times to get,

\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1+\varepsilon}.

It completes the proof of the averaged local law in
Q
in Theorem
S.6.1
under the additional assumption that
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal.

Next, we consider the averaged local law in
Q_{-}
. The follows exactly from the same arguments but with the specific form of
\Im\phi_{p}
when
E\leq\rho-n^{-2/3+a^{\prime}}
. When
E
is restricted to
E\leq\rho-n_{2}^{-2/3+a^{\prime}}
and
\eta\geq n_{2}^{-2/3}
, we have

\Im\phi_{p}\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}},

where
\kappa=\rho-E
. Assume that
\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}
. When
z\in Q_{-}
, Eq. (
S.6.9
) can be improved to

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{p}

\displaystyle\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}}{n_{2}\eta\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}+\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c}}\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}=\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)}+\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c}}\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta\sqrt{\kappa+\eta}}

\displaystyle\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)}+\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c+a^{\prime}/2}},

plugging the estimate of
\hat{\phi}_{p}
back to Eq. (
S.6.7
), we also have

\mathfrak{B}_{n}\prec\mathfrak{B}_{p}+\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)}+\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c+a^{\prime}/2}}.

All together, we get

\mathfrak{B}\prec\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c}}\quad\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\quad\mathfrak{B}\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)}+\frac{1}{(n_{2}\eta)^{c+a^{\prime}/2}}.

Again, from Proposition
S.6.1
,
\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-1/4}
. We iterate the equation finite number of times to get,

\mathfrak{B}\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}(\kappa+\eta)},

uniformly on
Q_{-}
. It completes the proof of the averaged local law in
Q_{-}
in Theorem
S.6.1
under the additional assumption that
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal.

Lastly, we consider the averaged local law in
Q_{\rm away}
. It follows from similar arguments to the previous two cases. On
Q_{\rm away}
, we invoke Lemma
S.5.7
to control
|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|
. Assuming
\mathfrak{B}\prec(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}
, then

\mathfrak{B}_{p}=|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|\leq C|\delta_{1}|+C|\delta_{2}|\prec\frac{\Im\phi_{p}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c}}{n_{2}\eta}\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c-1}\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}+(n_{2}\eta)^{-c-1}.

Here, we are using the fact that
\Im\phi_{p}/\eta\asymp 1
when
z
is away from the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
. The rest arguments are very similar to those in the previous two cases. We omit further details.

We completed the proof of the averaged local laws when
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal. The diagonal assumption will be removed in Section
S.6.1.5
.

S.6.1.4
The entrywise local law

It remains to prove the entrywise local law. Using Eq. (
S.6.2
), Result (v) of Lemma
S.6.1
, Lemma
S.6.4
and the averaged local law, we obtain

\mathbf{J}_{uu}-\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z+{\phi}_{p}}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}\prec\Psi,

uniformly for
u=1,\dots,p
and
z\in Q
.
Similarly, using Eq. (
S.6.3
),

\mathbf{J}_{ii}+\frac{1}{1+(p/n_{2})\phi_{p}}\prec\Psi,

uniformly for
i=p+1,\dots,p+n_{2}
oand
z\in Q
.
Moreover, since
\Lambda_{o}\prec\Psi_{\mathfrak{B}}\prec\Psi
, the entrywise local law follows.

S.6.1.5
Extension to non-diagonal
\Sigma_{p}

While the previous analysis assumes
\Sigma_{p}
is diagonal, in this section, we show that Theorem
S.6.1
and Theorem
S.6.2
hold when the population covariance matrix is non-diagonal. In order to utilize the notation in the previous sections, we denote the population covariance as
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
and continue to use
\Sigma_{p}
to denote the diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
. The eigendecomposition of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
is denoted as

\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}=H\Sigma_{p}H^{T},

where
H
is the eigenvector matrix of
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
. In this section, we show that if Theorem
S.6.1
holds with
\Sigma_{p}
, it also holds with
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
. All definitions in Section
S.6.1.1
are kept.

Define

\tilde{\mathbf{K}}=\left(\begin{matrix}H&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right)\mathbf{K}\left(\begin{matrix}H^{T}&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right),

\tilde{\mathbf{J}}=\left(\begin{matrix}H&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right)\mathbf{J}\left(\begin{matrix}H^{T}&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right),

\tilde{\Omega}=\left(\begin{matrix}H&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right)\Omega\left(\begin{matrix}H^{T}&0\\
0&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

Then,
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{K}}
and
\tilde{\Omega}
are equal to
\mathbf{K}
,
\mathbf{J}
and
\Omega
when
\Sigma_{p}
is replaced by
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
.

The averaged local laws in
Q
,
Q_{-}
and
Q_{\rm away}
still hold when
\mathbf{J}
and
\Omega
are replaced by
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}
and
\tilde{\Omega}
, since

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{u=1}^{p}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{uu}=\frac{1}{p}\mathrm{tr}(\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{(11)})=\frac{1}{p}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{J}_{(11)}H^{T}H)=\hat{\phi}_{p}.

It remains to show the entrywise local law. We shall actually show

H(\mathbf{J}_{(11)}-\Omega_{(11)})H^{T}=O_{\prec}(\Psi),

uniformly in
Q
. In components, this reads

\left|\sum_{u,v=1}^{p}H_{iu}\left(\mathbf{J}_{uv}-\mathbb{I}(u=v)\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-h_{p}}\right)H_{vj}\right|\prec\Psi,

for all
i,j\in\{1,2,\dots,p\}
.

The proof is based on the polynomialization method developed in Section 5 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
. The argument is very similar to that of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
, and we only outline the differences.

By the assumption

\mathbf{J}_{(11)}-(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-h_{p}I_{p})^{-1}=O_{\prec}(\Psi)

and orthogonality of
H
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{u,v=1}^{p}H_{iu}\left(\mathbf{J}_{uv}-\mathbb{I}(u=v)\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-h_{p}}\right)H_{vj}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u=1}^{p}H_{iu}\left(\mathbf{J}_{uu}-\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-h_{p}}\right)H_{uj}+\sum_{u\neq v}H_{iu}\mathbf{J}_{uv}H_{vj}=O_{\prec}(\Psi)+\mathcal{Z}

where we defined
\mathcal{Z}:=\sum_{u\neq v}H_{iu}\mathbf{J}_{uv}H_{vj}
. We need to prove that
|\mathcal{Z}|\prec\Psi
, which, following Section 5 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
we do by estimating the moment
\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{Z}|^{k}
for fixed
k\in 2\mathbb{N}
. The argument from Section 5 of
Bloemendal et al. (2014)
may be taken over with minor changes.

Use Result (ii) and (v) of Lemma
S.6.1
,

\sum_{u\neq v\notin S}H_{iu}\mathbf{J}_{uv}^{(S)}H_{vj}=\sum_{u\neq v\notin S}H_{iu}H_{vj}\mathbf{J}_{uu}^{(S)}\mathbf{J}_{vv}^{(S\cup\{u\})}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}^{(S\cup\{uv\})}_{(22)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}_{v\cdot}\right)

for any
S\subset\{1,2,\dots,p\}
and

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{uu}^{(S)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z-\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}_{(22)}^{(S\cup\{u\})}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}^{T}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=0}^{L-1}\frac{1}{(\lambda/\sigma_{up}-z+[1+(p/n_{2})\phi_{p}]^{-1})^{\ell+1}}\left(\left(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}\mathbf{J}^{(S\cup\{u\})}_{(22)}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{u\cdot}^{T}\right)-\frac{1}{1+(p/n_{2})\phi_{p}}\right)^{\ell}

\displaystyle\phantom{sssss}+O_{\prec}\left(\Psi^{L}\right).

We omit further details.

S.6.2
Proof of Theorem
S.6.2

The rigidity of the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
can be proved using the averaged local law in
Q_{-}
as in Theorem
S.6.1
. In the following,
\epsilon\in(0,2/3)
is fixed. Since
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}
is non-negative definite, all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
are therefore at least
\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
.
It then suffices to show that there is no eigenvalue in the interval

[\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p}),\rho_{p}-n_{2}^{-2/3+\epsilon}).

Recall the definition of
Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})
and
Q_{-}(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})
in Section
S.5.3
. We consider
a
and
a^{\prime}
to be sufficiently small so that

\Big\{z~:~E\in[\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p}),~\rho_{p}-n_{2}^{-2/3+\epsilon}],~~n_{2}^{-2/3}\leq\eta\leq 1\Big\}\subset Q_{-}.

Consider the set

Q^{*}=Q_{-}\cap\Big\{z\mid\eta=n_{2}^{-1/2-\epsilon/4}|E-\rho|^{1/4}\Big\}.

To show that there is no eigenvalue in
[\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p}),\rho-n_{2}^{-2/3+\epsilon})
, we only need to show that uniformly in
Q^{*}

\Im\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)\prec n_{2}^{-\epsilon/2}(n_{2}\eta)^{-1}.

It is because that
\hat{\phi}_{p}
is the Stieltjes transform of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
. When
z=\ell(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda})+\mathrm{i}\eta
where
\ell(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda})
is an eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
,

\Im\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)\asymp\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}.

Recall that in Lemma
S.5.4
for
z\in Q^{*}
,

\Im\phi_{p}\asymp\frac{\eta}{\sqrt{|E-\rho|+\eta}}.

We conclude that it suffices to prove

|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}(|E-\rho|+\eta)},

which is ensured by the averaged local law in
Q_{-}
in Theorem
S.6.1
.

S.6.3
Proof of Theorem
S.6.3

Theorem
S.6.2
indicates that the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
is larger than
\rho-\epsilon
with high probability. Lemma
S.6.3
indicates that the largest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
is smaller than

x_{\rm right}=(1+\sqrt{\hat{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}+\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})+\epsilon,

with high probability. Therefore, the statement in (i) of Theorem
S.6.3
holds.

The convergence of
p^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{p}f(\ell_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}))
can be proved using the strategy of
Bai and Silverstein (2004)
. We consider a contour in the complex plane that encloses the interval

[\rho-\epsilon,~(1+\sqrt{\hat{\gamma}_{2}})^{2}+\lambda/\liminf\ell_{\min}(\Sigma_{p})+\epsilon].

Without loss of generality, we choose the rectangle, denoted by
\mathcal{R}
, with the four vertices

z_{1}=\rho-2\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{i},~~z_{2}=\rho-2\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{i},~~z_{3}=x_{\rm right}+\epsilon+\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{i},~~z_{4}=x_{\rm right}+\epsilon-\epsilon^{\prime}\mathrm{i}.

In the following, we choose
\epsilon
and
\epsilon^{\prime}
to be sufficiently small so that the function
f
is analytic on the rectangle.

Clearly, with high probability, the rectangle encloses all eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
. Notably, if it is, we have

\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}f(\ell_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}))=\int f(\tau)dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\oint_{\mathcal{R}}f(z)\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)dz.

Here, the contour integral is taken over the rectangle in the positive direction in the complex plane. In the integral, we extend the definition of
\hat{\phi}_{p}
to
\mathbb{C}^{-}
by setting
\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)=\overline{\hat{\phi}_{p}(\overline{z})}
if
\eta(z)<0
.
To show the convergence of the linear spectral statistics, we only need to show

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{M})\oint_{\mathcal{R}}|\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-\phi_{p}(z)||dz|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

For any
c\in(0,1)
, we define

\hat{\mathcal{R}}(c)=\mathcal{R}\cap\{z:\eta\geq n_{2}^{-1+c}\}.

Then,

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{M})\oint_{\mathcal{R}\setminus\hat{\mathcal{R}}(c)}|\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)||dz|\leq Cn_{2}^{-1+c}.

Here, we are using the fact that when
\mathfrak{M}
holds, a deterministic bound on
|\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)|
holds uniformly for
z\in\mathcal{R}\setminus\hat{\mathcal{R}}(c)
.

Therefore, to show

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{M})\left|\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}f(\ell_{j}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}))-\int f(\tau)d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)\right|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

We only need to show that for an arbitrary
c\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{I}(\mathfrak{M})\oint_{\hat{\mathcal{R}}(c)}|\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-\phi_{p}(z)||dz|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}},

It is a direct consequence of the averaged local law on
Q_{\rm away}
in Theorem
S.6.1
. It completes the proof.

S.6.4
Proof of Theorem
S.6.4

Although the extension of the local laws to non-Gaussian settings is technically more demanding, a general framework for this purpose has been developed in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. Theorem
S.6.4
can be established by following the arguments in Sections 7–9 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
in a largely verbatim manner. Accordingly, in this section, we only describe the similarities and differences, and highlight the modifications required to adapt the proofs in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
to our setting.

In
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, concentration of the resolvent matrix

{\mathbf{J}}_{KY}(z)={\mathbf{K}}_{KY}^{-1}(z),\qquad{\mathbf{K}}_{KY}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma^{-1}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\mathbf{{Z}}\\[7.0pt]
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{2}}}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}&-zI_{n_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

around its deterministic equivalent

{\Omega}_{KY}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}-\Sigma_{p}(1+m_{p}(z)\Sigma_{p})^{-1}&0\\
0&m_{p}(z)I_{n_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

is established. Here,
m_{p}(z)
is defined analogously to
q(z)
in Section
S.5.2
, with
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{1}
replaced by
F^{\Sigma_{p}}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
, respectively. In particular,
Knowles and Yin (2017)
prove the local law

{\mathbf{J}}_{KY}(z)-{\Omega}_{KY}(z)=O_{\prec}\!\left({\Psi}_{KY}(z)\right),

for
z
in a neighborhood of the support edge of the measure associated with
m_{p}(z)
, where the control parameter is

{\Psi}_{KY}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\Im m_{p}(z)}{n_{2}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}.

For any deterministic unit vectors
v
and
w
, define the inner product function

F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}})=F_{KY}(z,v,w)=|v^{T}\!\left(\mathbf{J}_{KY}(z)-\Omega_{KY}(z)\right)w|,

where the resolvent is constructed from the observations
\mathbf{{Z}}
. It suffices to show that

F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}})\prec\Psi_{KY}(z)

whenever
z
lies near the edge of the measure associated with
m_{p}(z)
, for any fixed sequences of unit vectors
v
and
w
, and for any
\mathbf{{Z}}
satisfying Condition
C2
.

The argument proceeds by first establishing the result under Gaussian observations. Let
\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}
denote a matrix with i.i.d.
N(0,1)
entries. It then suffices to control the difference

\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}})-\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}),

where
\mathbf{{Z}}
satisfies Condition
C2
. To this end,
Knowles and Yin (2017)
employ an interpolation method in which a family of distributions
\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in[0,1]\}
is introduced such that
f_{0}
is
N(0,1)
and
f_{1}
is the distribution of the entries of
\mathbf{{Z}}
(see Definition 7.6 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
for details). Let
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
denote the matrix whose entries follow the distribution
f_{\theta}
, and denote by
\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
,
\mathbf{K}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
,
F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
the corresponding resolvent, linearization matrix and the inner-product function.
To control
\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{1})-\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0})
, one analyzes the change in
\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
induced by replacing a single entry of
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
. Specifically, let
\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,s}
denote the matrix obtained from
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
by replacing its
(u,v)
-th entry with the value
s
. It turns out that, to control
\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{1})-\mathbb{E}F_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0})
, we only need to control

\sum_{(u,v)}\mathbb{E}F_{KY}^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbb{E}F_{KY}^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}),

for arbitrary power
k\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, and
(u,v)
. See Lemmas 7.9 and 7.10 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
for details. The analysis relies on explicitly quantifying the difference in
\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
induced by the change of the
(u,v)
-th entry of
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
. Since
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=-A^{-1}(A-B)B^{-1}
, we have

\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})[\mathbf{K}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{K}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})]\mathbf{J}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}).

\mathbf{K}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{K}_{KY}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-(z_{uv}^{1}-z_{uv}^{0})[e_{(p+n_{2})u}e_{n_{2}v}^{T}\Delta^{T}+\Delta e_{n_{2}v}e_{(p+n_{2})u}^{T}].

(S.6.11)

Here,
e_{pi}
is the standard unit vector of dimension
p
in the coordinate direction
i
and

\Delta=(0_{n_{2}\times p},~~~~n_{2}^{-1/2}I_{n_{2}})^{T}.

Arguments in Sections 7–9 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
are mainly devoted to the study of the above quantities.

Under our context, we consider the concentration of the resolvent matrix

\mathbf{J}(z)=\mathbf{K}^{-1}(z),\qquad\mathbf{K}(z)=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda\Sigma^{-1}-zI_{p}&\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\\[7.0pt]
U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}&-I_{n_{2}}\end{pmatrix}

to the deterministic equivalent

\Omega(z)=\left(\begin{matrix}\left(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-h_{p}(z)I_{p}\right)^{-1}&0\\
0&(z-h_{p}(z))I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right),

when
z
is near the edge of the support of the probability distribution associated with
\phi_{p}(z)
(specifically,
z\in Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})
).

Similarly, we define the inner product function

F(\mathbf{{Z}})=F(z,v,w)=|v^{T}\!\left(\mathbf{J}(z)-\Omega(z)\right)w|,

It suffices to show that

F(\mathbf{{Z}})\prec\Psi(z)

for any fixed sequences of unit vectors
v
and
w
, and for any
\mathbf{{Z}}
satisfying Condition
C2
.

Similar to the framework in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, the argument proceeds by first establishing the result under Gaussian observations, which is completed in Theorem
S.6.1
. The results are then extended to non-Gaussianity by controlling the difference

\mathbb{E}F(\mathbf{{Z}})-\mathbb{E}F(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}).

Following the framework in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, we only need to control

\sum_{uv}\mathbb{E}F^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbb{E}F^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}),

for arbitrary power
k\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, and
(u,v)
. While
\mathbf{J}
differs from
\mathbf{J}_{KY}
in all four blocks, we have similar stochastic domination bounds on

\|\mathbf{J}(z)\|\quad\text{and}\quad\|\mathbf{J}(z)-\Omega(z)\|,

as that on
\|\mathbf{J}_{KY}(z)\|
and
\|\mathbf{J}_{KY}(z)-\Omega_{KY}(z)\|
. Moreover, analogously to those for
\mathbf{J}_{KY}
,

\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})[\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})]\mathbf{J}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}).

\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{K}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-(z_{uv}^{1}-z_{uv}^{0})[e_{(p+n_{2})u}e_{n_{0}v}^{T}\Delta^{T}_{\rm new}+\Delta_{\rm new}e_{n_{0}v}e_{(p+n_{2})u}^{T}],

(S.6.12)

with the updated matrix

\Delta_{\rm new}=(0_{n_{0}\times p},~~~~U_{2})^{T}.

Here, we only need to substitute
U_{2}
for
I_{n_{2}}
.

It is not hard to verify that all bounds in Sections 7–9 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
remain valid under this updated definition of
\Delta_{\rm new}
. Therefore, their arguments apply in a largely verbatim manner, with the only necessary change being the substitution of
\Delta_{\rm new}
. This change effectively corresponds to replacing the data matrix “
X
” in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
by
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
. Similar substitution of
\Delta_{\rm new}
is conducted in
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
(See (9.30) and (9.31) of
Han et al. (2018)
). We omit further details.

S.6.5
Discrete case

While the previous subsections, we focus on Case (a), namely when
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
, in this section, we consider the case when
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
.
We show analogous results as Theorem
S.6.1
, Theorem
S.6.2
and Theorem
S.6.3
.

Theorem S.6.5
.

Suppose that
C1
–
C6
. Assume that
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. Then, with probability
1
, the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
.

Theorem S.6.6
.

Suppose the conditions in Theorem
S.6.5
hold. Then, uniformly in
Q_{\rm away}

\hat{\phi}_{p}(z)-{\phi}_{p}(z)=O_{\prec}(\frac{1}{n_{2}}).

However, it is worth noting that the local law in
Q(a,a^{\prime},n_{2})
as in Theorem
S.6.1
no longer holds, since
\Im\phi_{p}(z)
diverges as
z\to\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
.

Theorem S.6.7
.

Suppose the conditions in Theorem
S.6.5
hold. The results in Theorem
S.6.3
still hold.

Under Definition
2.2
, for all sufficiently large
p
, we have

pF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\{\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})\})>n_{2}.

Note that
pF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\{\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})\})
is the rank of the eigen-subspace associated with the smallest eigenvalue of
\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}
, that is
\lambda\ell^{-1}_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
. The rank of
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}
is
n_{2}
with probability
1
, since the condition implies that
p>n_{2}
. Therefore, using Weyl’s inequality, we obtain that there are at least one eigenvalue of
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}
less than or equal to
\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
. On the other hand,
\ell_{\min}(\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1})\geq\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
. Therefore, with probability 1, the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
is
\lambda/\ell_{\max}(\Sigma_{p})
.
∎

The proof of Theorem
S.6.6
follows the same arguments as those used in the proof of Theorem
S.6.1
. The only modification is to replace the bound in Lemma
S.5.6
with the stronger bound provided in Lemma
S.5.7
. We therefore omit the details.
∎

The proof of Theorem
S.6.7
follows the same arguments as those used in the proof of Theorem
S.6.3
. The bound on

|\hat{\phi}_{p}-\phi_{p}|

in the final step is now ensured by Theorem
S.6.6
, rather than by Theorem
S.6.1
.
. ∎

S.7
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Recall the proof outline presented in the main text. We begin by establishing the result under the Gaussian assumption and subsequently extend it to the non-Gaussian setting. Step 1 has already been completed in Section
S.6
. In this section, we establish the remaining steps.

S.7.1
Step 2: Tracy–Widom Limit under Gaussianity

Under Gaussianity, the matrices
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}
and
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
are independent. It is therefore equivalent to consider a matrix of the form

\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{1},\qquad\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}=\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{2}\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{2}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1},

where
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{1}
is a
p\times n_{1}
matrix with i.i.d.
N(0,1/n_{1})
entries,
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{2}
is a
p\times n_{2}
matrix with i.i.d.
N(0,1/n_{2})
entries, and
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{{Z}}}_{2}
are independent.

The main strategy of the proof is as follows. Conditioning on
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
, we apply the main theorem of
Lee and Schnelli (2016)
, treating
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}
as the population covariance matrix (corresponding to
\Sigma
in their notation). To justify this application, it remains to verify that the regularity condition in Assumption 2.2 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
holds with high probability.

For the reader’s convenience, we restate here the key quantities introduced in Section
S.5.2
. Recall the function

f(q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}s(-q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau+q}.

Then,
-\beta
is the critical point of
f
in
(-\rho,0)
. Namely,

0=f^{\prime}(-\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}s^{\prime}(\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{(\tau-\beta)^{2}}.

Also, the centering parameter
\Theta_{1}=\Theta_{1p}
is

\Theta_{1}=f(-\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}s(\beta)=\frac{1}{\beta}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau-\beta}.

Moreover, recall the definition of the scaling parameter
\Theta_{2}=\Theta_{2p}
as

{\Theta}_{2}=\left[\big(\frac{p}{n_{1}}\big)^{3}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{(\tau-{\beta})^{3}}+\frac{(p/n_{1})^{2}}{{\beta}^{3}}\right]^{1/3}.

The following local domain close to
\Theta_{1}
is considered.

{D}={D}(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})\coloneqq\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~:~|E-{\Theta}_{1}|\leq a,~~n_{1}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\leq\eta\leq 1/a^{\prime}\}.

Results in Section
S.6
indicate that the LSS of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
converges to their deterministic counterparts based on
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
and the smallest eigenvalue of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
converges to
\rho
. We replace
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
with the stochastic ESD
F^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}
and define the following counterparts of
f(\cdot)
,
\beta
, and
\Theta_{1}
.

Denote the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
to be
g_{1}\geq g_{2}\geq\cdots\geq g_{p}\geq\lambda/\sigma_{1p}>0
. Replacing
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
with
F^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}
, we define

\hat{f}(q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)}{\tau+q}=-\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{g_{j}+q}.

Then, there exists a unique solution to the equation
z=\hat{f}(q)
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, we denote it to be
\hat{q}(z)
. Namely,

z=-\frac{1}{\hat{q}}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)}{\tau+\hat{q}}.

(S.7.1)

The unique critical point of
\hat{f}(q)
in
(-g_{p},0)
is denoted as
-\hat{\beta}
. Namely,

0=\hat{f}^{\prime}(-\hat{\beta})=\frac{1}{\hat{\beta}^{2}}-\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{(g_{j}-\hat{\beta})^{2}}.

It is straightforward to verify that
\hat{\beta}
exists and is unique for any fixed sequence
g_{j}
’s. Let

\hat{\Theta}_{1}=\hat{f}(-\hat{\beta})=\frac{1}{\hat{\beta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{g_{j}-\hat{\beta}}.

Lastly, call

\hat{\Theta}_{2}=\left[({\frac{p}{n_{1}})^{3}}\int\frac{dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)}{(\tau-\hat{\beta})^{3}}+\frac{(p/n_{1})^{2}}{\hat{\beta}^{3}}\right]^{1/3}.

The following results are derived from those in Section
S.6
.

Theorem S.7.1
.

Suppose that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. Additionally, suppose that the observations are Gaussian.

(i)

There exists a universal constant
c>0
such that
|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c
holds with high probability.

(ii)

|\hat{\Theta}_{1}-\Theta_{1}|\prec 1/n_{2}
and
|\hat{\Theta}_{2}-\Theta_{2}|\prec 1/n_{2}

(iii)

For arbitrary
a^{\prime}>0
, there exists
a>0
such that

|\hat{q}(z)-q(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},\qquad z\in D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1}).

Before presenting the proof of Theorem
S.7.1
, we first explain how Theorem
S.7.1
implies that Theorem
2.2
holds under Gaussianity.

Fix
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
and suppose that
|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c
holds for some small constant
c
. Then, the condition in Assumption 2.2 of
Lee and Schnelli (2016)
is satisfied. Then, applying the main theorem of
Lee and Schnelli (2016)
, we obtain that, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{p\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\frac{p^{2/3}}{\hat{\Theta}_{2}}\Big(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})-\hat{\Theta}_{1}\Big)\leq t\right\}\cap\left\{|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c\right\}\mid\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{TW}_{1}(t),

where
\mathbb{TW}_{1}(t)
is the distribution function of the Tracy-Widom distribution of type 1. Now, integrating both sides with respect to the distribution of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
, using the dominated convergence theorem, we obtain that, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\lim_{p\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left\{\frac{p^{2/3}}{\hat{\Theta}_{2}}\Big(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})-\hat{\Theta}_{1}\Big)\leq t\right\}\cap\left\{|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c\right\}\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{TW}_{1}(t).

Using the results of Theorem
S.7.1
, we can select the constant
c
to be sufficiently small so that
\mathbb{P}(|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c)\to 1
. Also, by the convergence of
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}
and Slutsky’s theorem, the weak convergence still holds when
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}
are replaced by
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
. It completes the proof of Theorem
2.2
under Gaussianity.

The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem
S.7.1
.

We separately consider two cases:
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
and
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. For both cases, since
s^{\prime}(x)\to\infty
as
x\to\rho
, we have that
\beta<\rho-c
for some universal constant
c>0
and all sufficiently large
p
. We first consider the case when
\rho>\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. The second case is rather straightforward, and we address it at the end.

Theorem
S.6.2
in Section
S.6
indicates that

|g_{p}-\rho|\prec n_{2}^{-2/3}.

In order to show
|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|\geq c
with high probability for some constant
c
, it suffices to show that

|\hat{\beta}-\beta|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

Recall the functions
f(q)
and
\hat{f}(q)
. We extend the functions to complex-valued
q
. In particular, for
q\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup(-\rho,0)
, we consider
f^{\prime}(q)
,
f^{\prime\prime}(q)
,
\hat{f}^{\prime}(q)
, and
\hat{f}^{\prime\prime}(q)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{2}}-\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{(\tau+q)^{2}},

\displaystyle f^{\prime\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{q^{3}}+2\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{(\tau+q)^{3}},

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{2}}-\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\frac{1}{(g_{p}+q)^{2}},

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{\prime\prime}(q)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{q^{3}}+\frac{2}{n_{1}}\sum\frac{1}{(g_{j}+q)^{3}}.

Since
\beta
is away from
\rho
, there exists a domain

\mathfrak{D}=\{z~:~|E+\beta|\leq C,~~0\leq\eta\leq C^{\prime}\},\quad\text{for some }C,C^{\prime}>0

such that

\left|f^{\prime\prime}(q)\right|\asymp 1,\quad q\in\mathfrak{D}.

In the following, we shall always select
C
and
C^{\prime}
to be such that the condition is satisfied.

We notice that in
(-g_{p},0)
,
\hat{f}^{\prime\prime}(q)>0
. Therefore, there exists at most one critical point of
\hat{f}(q)
on
(-g_{p},0)
. To show that
|\hat{\beta}-\beta|\prec n_{2}^{-1}
, we only need to show that for arbitrary small
\epsilon>0
, there exists a root to
\hat{f}(q)=0
in
(-\beta-n_{1}^{-1+\epsilon},-\beta+n_{1}^{-1+\epsilon})
with high probability.

Clearly, due to the boundedness of
f^{\prime\prime}(q)
in
\mathfrak{D}
, we can find constants
C_{1}>0
and
C_{2}>0
such that

C_{1}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\Re f^{\prime}(-\beta+n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2})\leq C_{2}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon},

-C_{2}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\Re f^{\prime}(-\beta-n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2})\leq-C_{1}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon},

\Im f^{\prime}(-\beta\pm n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2})\asymp n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2}.

For any fixed
q\in\mathfrak{D}
, we view

f(q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\int\frac{1}{\tau+q}d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)=\int(-1/q)\tau+\frac{\hat{\gamma}_{1}}{\tau+q}d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau),

as a LSS with the analytical function being
(-1/q)\tau+\hat{\gamma}_{1}(\tau+q)^{-1}
. The condition of Theorem
S.6.3
is satisfied.
Applying Theorem
S.6.3
, we obtain that

\mathbb{I}(g_{p}\geq(\rho+\beta)/2)\left|\hat{f}(-\beta\pm n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2})-f(-\beta\pm n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}+\mathrm{i}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon/2})\right|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

Here, we also use the uniformity of the stochastic domination across all LSS
f(q)
when
q\in\mathfrak{D}
. It is easy to verify by checking the arguments in the proof of Theorem
S.6.3
. Indeed, the domination is uniform for all
\{f_{q},q\in\mathfrak{D}\}
if a universal bound on
\sup_{q}|1/q|
and
\sup_{q}\sup_{\tau\in(\rho-a,\infty)}(\tau+q)^{-1}
when
q\in\mathfrak{D}
exists for some sufficiently small
a>0
.

Moreover, since when
g_{p}\geq(\rho+\beta/2)
and
q\in\mathfrak{D}
,
|\hat{f}^{\prime\prime}(q)|\asymp 1
.
We conclude that there exists constants
C_{1}>0
and
C_{2}>0
such that with high probability

C_{1}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\hat{f}^{\prime}(-\beta+n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon})\leq C_{2}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon},

-C_{2}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}\leq\hat{f}^{\prime}(-\beta-n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon})\leq-C_{1}n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}.

It implies that there exists a critical point of
\hat{f}^{\prime}(q)
in between
-\beta\pm n_{2}^{-1+\epsilon}
with high probability. It completes the proof of Result (i).

To show Result (ii), first observe that when
g_{p}\geq(\rho+\beta)/2
,

\hat{\Theta}_{1}-\Theta_{1}=\hat{f}(-\hat{\beta})-f(-\beta)=(\hat{f}(-\hat{\beta})-\hat{f}(-\beta))+(\hat{f}(-\beta)-f(-\beta)).

The first term is such that

\hat{f}(-\hat{\beta})-\hat{f}(-\beta)=O_{\prec}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)=O_{\prec}(\frac{1}{n_{2}}).

Here, we are using the fact that
\hat{f}^{\prime}(q)
is bounded uniformly when
q\in\mathfrak{D}
with high probability. For the second term, we again treat
\hat{f}(-\beta)
as a LSS of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
. Using Theorem
S.6.3
,

\mathbb{I}(g_{p}\geq(\rho+\beta)/2)|\hat{f}(-\beta)-f(-\beta)|\prec n_{2}^{-1}.

It follows that
|\hat{\Theta}_{1}-\Theta_{1}|\prec 1/n_{2}
.

Moreover, we can verify that
|\hat{\Theta}_{2}-\Theta_{2}|\prec 1/n_{2}
following similar results. We omit details.

Lastly, we show Result (iii).
Recall that

z=-\frac{1}{\hat{q}(z)}+(p/n_{1})\int\frac{dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)}{\tau+\hat{q}(z)}.

Fix
z_{0}=E+\mathrm{i}\eta
such that
\eta\geq 1
. Applying Lemma
S.5.1
, we obtain that

\Im\hat{q}(z_{0})\geq C,

for some universal constant
C>0
independent of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
. Therefore,
-\hat{q}(z_{0})
is away from the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
. Applying the local laws of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
away from the support as in Theorem
S.6.1
, we obtain that

\left|\int\frac{dF^{\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}}(\tau)}{\tau+\hat{q}(z_{0})}-\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau+\hat{q}(z_{0})}\right|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

It follows then

z_{0}+O_{\prec}(n_{1}^{-1})=-\frac{1}{\hat{q}(z_{0})}+(p/n_{1})\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau+\hat{q}(z_{0})}.

Due to the strong stability of the Marčenko-Pastur equation (Lemma
S.5.2
), we obtain

|\hat{q}(z)-q(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}}.

To propagate the convergence from
\eta\geq 1
to all
z\in D
, we then use a stochastic continuity argument and Lemma
S.5.2
to propagate the smallness of
|\hat{q}_{p}(z)-q(z)|
. The argument is very similar to those in the proof of Proposition
S.6.1
. We therefore omit the details. All together, we conclude that

|\hat{q}(z)-q(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}.

As for the case when
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
, from Theorem
S.6.5
, Result (i) clearly holds. Results (ii) and (iii) follow from the same arguments as in the case of
\rho=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
. We only need to replace the arguments in Theorem
S.6.3
with that in Theorem
S.6.7
.

∎

S.7.2
Step 3: Green Function Comparison Theorem and Linearization Matrix

While the asymptotic Tracy–Widom distribution under Gaussianity has been established in Steps 1 and 2, the goal of the present step is to extend the result to non-Gaussian distributions satisfying Condition
C2
. This phenomenon is known as edge universality in the RMT literature, meaning that the asymptotic distribution of edge eigenvalues is typically unaffected by the specific distribution of the matrix entries, provided that suitable moment conditions are satisfied.

Edge universality is typically established via a Green function comparison theorem. Define the resolvent (Green function) of the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda}
by

\mathbf{L}(z)=(U_{1}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}-zI_{n_{1}})^{-1},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

We consider two settings: one in which
\mathbf{{Z}}
has a general distribution satisfying Condition
C2
, and one in which
\mathbf{{Z}}
is Gaussian. To distinguish the latter from the former, we denote the Gaussian matrix by
\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}
. Accordingly, the resolvent corresponding to
\mathbf{{Z}}
is denoted by
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}
, while the resolvent corresponding to
\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}
is denoted by
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}
.
The averaged trace of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(z)
and
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}
is taken as

\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(z)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(z))\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(z)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(z)).

Moreover, the transformed F matrix corresponding to
\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}
is denoted by
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda}
.

Theorem S.7.2
(Green function comparison).

Let
\epsilon>0
and set
\eta=p^{-2/3-\epsilon}
. Let
E_{1},E_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
E_{1}<E_{2}
and

|E_{1}-\Theta_{1}|,\;|E_{2}-\Theta_{1}|\leq p^{-2/3+\epsilon}.

Let
K:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a smooth function such that

\max_{x}|K^{(l)}(x)|\leq C,\qquad l=1,2,3,4,5,

for some constant
C>0
. Then there exists a constant
c>0
such that, for all sufficiently large
p
and sufficiently small
\epsilon
,

\left|\mathbb{E}\,K\!\left(p\int_{E_{1}}^{E_{2}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(x+i\eta)\,dx\right)-\mathbb{E}\,K\!\left(p\int_{E_{1}}^{E_{2}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(x+i\eta)\,dx\right)\right|\leq p^{-c}.

The theorem is a standard result in RMT; See, for example, Theorem 6.3 of
Erdős et al. (2012)
. To connect Theorem
S.7.2
to the edge universality of
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda}
, we need the following results.

Lemma S.7.1
(Rigidity of largest eigenvalue).

Under Conditions
C1
–
C6
, we have

\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})-\Theta_{1}=O_{\prec}(p^{-2/3}).

Given Lemma
S.7.1
, we can locate to the domain of
E
such that
|E-\Theta_{1}|\prec p^{-2/3}
. Fix
E^{*}\prec p^{-2/3}
such that it suffices to consider
\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})\leq\Theta_{1}+E^{*}
. Choose
|E-\Theta_{1}|\prec p^{-2/3}
,
\eta=p^{-2/3-9\epsilon}
and
l=\frac{1}{2}p^{-2/3-\epsilon}
. Then, for some sufficiently small constant
\epsilon>0
and sufficiently large constant
M
, there exists a constant
p_{0}
depending on
\epsilon
and
M
, such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}K\left(\frac{n_{1}}{\pi}\int_{E-l}^{\Theta_{1}+E^{*}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(x+i\eta)dx\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})\leq E\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{ssssss}\leq\mathbb{E}K\left(\frac{n_{1}}{\pi}\int_{E+l}^{\Theta_{1}+E^{*}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(x+i\eta)dx\right)+n_{1}^{-M},

(S.7.2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}K\left(\frac{n_{1}}{\pi}\int_{E-l}^{\Theta_{1}+E^{*}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(x+i\eta)dx\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E\right)

\displaystyle\phantom{ssssss}\leq\mathbb{E}K\left(\frac{n_{1}}{\pi}\int_{E+l}^{\Theta_{1}+E^{*}}\Im\overline{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(x+i\eta)dx\right)+n_{1}^{-M},

(S.7.3)

whenever
p>p_{0}
and
K
is a smooth cutoff function satisfying the condition in Theorem
S.7.2
. We omit the details in the function
K
and the proof of Eq. (
S.7.2
) and Eq. (
S.7.3
) because it is a standard procedure in RMT. Similar works include Corollary 5.1 of Lemma 6.1 of
Erdős et al. (2012)
,
Bao et al. (2013)
, and Lemma 4.1 of
Pillai and Yin (2014)
.

Following Eq. (
S.7.2
) and Eq. (
S.7.3
), setting
E
in Eq. (
S.7.3
) as
E\pm 2l
, we immediately get

\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})\leq E)\geq\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E-2l)-n_{1}^{-M},

\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})\leq E)\leq\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E+2l)+n_{1}^{-M}.

Since
l=O(p^{-2/3-\epsilon})
, we have then

\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E\pm 2l)-\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E)\to 0.

We then conclude that

\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})\leq E)-\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})\leq E)\to 0.

Since we already established the asymptotic Tracy-Widom distribution of
\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}^{0}_{\lambda})
as stated in Theorem
2.2
,
\ell_{\max}(\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda})
has the same weak limit. It will complete the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

It remains to prove Lemma
S.7.1
and Theorem
S.7.2
. To this end, we analyze the behavior of the resolvent
\mathbf{L}(z)
in a neighborhood of
\Theta_{1}
. The objective is to show that the resolvent
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}}(z)
has the same asymptotic limit as that of
\mathbf{L}_{\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}}(z)
. However, it is difficult to work directly with
\mathbf{L}(z)
due to its complicated dependence on the individual entries of
\mathbf{{Z}}
.

Following the strategy of
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
, we instead express the resolvent
\mathbf{L}(z)
as a submatrix of the inverse of a linearization matrix.
In particular, define the following
3\times 3
block matrix

\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(z)=\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(z,\mathbf{{Z}})=\left(\begin{matrix}-zI_{n_{1}}&U_{1}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}&0\\[10.0pt]
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}&-\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}&\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\\[10.0pt]
0&U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}&I_{n_{2}}\end{matrix}\right).

(S.7.4)

Note that
\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(z)
is linear in
\mathbf{{Z}}
. Using the Schur complement formula, it can be verified easily that the upper-left block of the matrix
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)
is the resolvent
\mathbf{L}(z)
. Therefore, it suffices to study the properties of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)
. This linearization technique is widely used in RMT for studying the universality of matrices with complex structure.

It is important to note that when
\lambda=0
, the matrix
\mathbf{H}_{0}(z)
reduces to the linearization matrix (also denoted by
\mathbf{H}
) used in
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
. In other words, our linearization matrix differs from that in
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
only in a single deterministic block (the middle block). Owing to this structural similarity, the arguments developed in
Han et al. (2018)
can be reused with only minor modifications, which substantially simplifies our proof.

The explicit expression of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}(z)
can be obtained using the standard block matrix inversion formula

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{K}&\mathbf{B}\\
\mathbf{C}&\mathbf{D}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}\\
-\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}\end{pmatrix}\left(\mathbf{K}-\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{C}\right)^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}~&~\mathbf{B}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

As an illustration, the three diagonal blocks of
\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}^{-1}
are given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{11}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}(z)=(U_{1}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}-zI_{n_{1}})^{-1},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{22}

\displaystyle=(-\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+z^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}U_{1}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T})^{-1},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{33}

\displaystyle=I_{n_{2}}+U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{22}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}.

We omit the explicit expressions for the off-diagonal blocks.

Importantly, when
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
is regarded as fixed, all blocks of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}
can be expressed as linear combinations of the block matrices appearing in equation (4.3) of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, provided that the matrix

\mathbf{G}^{-1}_{\lambda}=(\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1})^{-1}

is taken as the population covariance matrix “
\Sigma
” in the formulation of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. For example, among the diagonal blocks,
(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{11}
corresponds to the last block in (4.3) of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
(denoted there by
G_{n}
), while
(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{22}
corresponds to the first block in (4.3) (denoted there by
G_{M}
). Finally,
(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{33}
can be written as
I_{n_{2}}+U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}G_{M}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
.

While the formal proof of the local laws for
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}
is presented in the next section, we briefly describe the conjectured limiting behavior of the blocks of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}
. Assume that the observations are Gaussian, so that
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}
and
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
are independent. Conditional on
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
, we may therefore apply the deterministic equivalents of
G_{M}
and
G_{N}
established in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. In particular, based on the form of the deterministic equivalents given in equation (3.5) of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{11}

\displaystyle\approx\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)\,I_{n_{1}},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{22}

\displaystyle\approx-\,\mathbf{G}^{-1}_{\lambda}\bigl(1+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}\bigr)^{-1}=-\,(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{33}

\displaystyle\approx I_{n_{2}}-U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{ij}

\displaystyle\approx 0,\quad\text{if }i\neq j.

Here,
\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)
is defined in (
S.7.1
).

Moreover,
(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}
is precisely the resolvent of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
evaluated at
-\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)
. Equivalently, it is the first block
\mathbf{J}_{(11)}
of
\mathbf{J}
evaluated in
-\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)
(see Eq. (
S.6.1
) and Lemma
S.6.1
). In addition, the matrix

I_{n_{2}}-U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}

can be written as
-\mathbf{J}_{(22)}(-\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z))
, where
\mathbf{J}_{(22)}
denotes the lower-right block of the matrix
\mathbf{J}
. Combining the deterministic equivalent
\Omega(z)
of
\mathbf{J}(z)
established in Section
S.6
with the concentration of
\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)
to
q_{\lambda}(z)
, we obtain

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{22}

\displaystyle\approx-\,(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}\approx\big(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-h_{\lambda}(-q_{\lambda}(z))I_{p}\big)^{-1},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda})_{33}

\displaystyle\approx I_{n_{2}}-U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\approx\big(h_{\lambda}(-q_{\lambda}(z))-z\big)I_{n_{2}}.

We define the following two block diagonal matrices

\displaystyle\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)=\widehat{\Pi}(z,\mathbf{{Z}},\lambda)=\left(\begin{matrix}\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{n_{1}}&0&0\\
0&(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}&0\\
0&0&I_{n_{2}}-U_{2}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}(\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}+\hat{q}_{\lambda}(z)I_{p})^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\end{matrix}\right).

\displaystyle\Pi_{\lambda}(z)=\Pi(z,\lambda)=\begin{pmatrix}q(z)I_{n_{1}}&0&0\\
0&\big(\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}-h_{\lambda}(-q_{\lambda}(z))I_{p}\big)^{-1}&0\\
0&&\big(h_{\lambda}(-q_{\lambda}(z))-z\big)I_{n_{2}}\end{pmatrix}.

It is worth noting that when
\lambda=0
, the matrix
\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z)
coincides with the “limit” matrix
\Pi(z)
in
Han et al. (2018)
(see equation (8.5) therein). In particular, for
\lambda=0
,
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
established that

\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{0}(z)-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z)\prec\sqrt{\frac{\Im q_{0}(z)}{n_{1}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

whenever
z
lies in a neighborhood of
\Theta_{1}(0)
. In the next section, we establish the corresponding concentration result for
\lambda>0
, where
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)
is replaced by the deterministic equivalent matrix
\Pi_{\lambda}(z)
.

S.7.3
Step 4: Local Laws for the Linearization Matrix

In this section, we establish a local law for the behavior of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)
when
z
is close to
\Theta_{1}
. As before, we frequently suppress the explicit dependence of many quantities on
\lambda
and
p
whenever no ambiguity arises.

Recall the domain

D=D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})=\Big\{z=E+\mathrm{i}\eta\in\mathbb{C}^{+}:|E-\Theta_{1}|\leq a,\;n_{1}^{-1+a^{\prime}}\leq\eta\leq 1/a^{\prime}\Big\}

from Section
S.5.3
. We define the control parameter

\Phi(z)=\Phi(z,\lambda)=\sqrt{\frac{\Im q_{\lambda}(z)}{n_{1}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta}.

Again, we proceed by first assume Gaussianity. The following theorem follows from a combination of Theorem 3.14 in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
and
S.7.1
in Section
S.7.1
.

Theorem S.7.3
.

Suppose that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. Additionally, assume that the observations are Gaussian. Fix any
\lambda>0
. For any
a^{\prime}>0
, there exist constants
a>0
such that the following results hold.

(i)

(Entrywise local law) We have

\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)-\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)=O_{\prec}(\Phi(z)),

uniformly in
{D}(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
.

(ii)

(Averaged local law) We have

|\overline{L}(z)-{q}_{\lambda}(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

uniformly in
D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. Here
\overline{L}(z)
is the averaged trace of
\mathbf{L}_{\lambda}(z)=(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z))_{11}
.

(iii)

(Local law for
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)
) Moreover,

\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)-\Pi_{\lambda}(z)=O_{\prec}(\frac{1}{n_{2}\eta}),

uniformly in
{D}(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. It immediately follows that part (i) still holds if
\widehat{\Pi}_{\lambda}(z)
is replaced by
\Pi_{\lambda}(z)
.

Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem
S.7.3
, as a connection to the literature,
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
established part (i) and (ii) of Theorem
S.7.3
when
\lambda=0
under both Gaussian and Condition
C2
.

By part (i) of Theorem
S.7.1
, there exists a small constant
c>0
such that
\mathbb{I}(|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c)=1
with high probability. Consequently, when
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}^{-1}
is regarded as the “population covariance matrix”, the regularity condition in Definition 2.7 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
is satisfied with high probability.

As explained in Section
S.7.2
, under this regularity condition, each block of
\mathbf{H}^{-1}
can be expressed as a linear combination of the blocks of the linearization matrix
G
studied in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
(see equation (4.3) therein). Applying the entrywise local laws from Theorem 3.14 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
, we obtain that for any
a^{\prime}>0
there exists
2a>0
such that

\mathbb{I}(|\hat{\beta}-g_{p}|>c)\,\big|\mathbf{H}^{-1}(z)-\widehat{\Pi}(z)\big|\,\Big|\,\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\prec\sqrt{\frac{\Im\hat{q}(z)}{n_{1}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

uniformly for
z\in\hat{D}(2a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. Here,
\hat{D}(2a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
denotes the
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
–dependent counterpart of
D(2a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
obtained by replacing
\Theta_{1}
with
\hat{\Theta}_{1}
. The notation
\xi\,|\,\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\prec\zeta
means that
\xi
is stochastically dominated by
\zeta
(Definition
S.5.2
) with respect to the conditional distribution of
\mathbf{{Z}}
given
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
.

Next, by part (ii) of Theorem
S.7.1
, for any
a>0
we have
D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})\subset\hat{D}(2a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
with high probability. Moreover, part (iii) of Theorem
S.7.1
implies that
|\hat{q}(z)-q(z)|\prec 1/(n_{1}\eta)
. Combining these results,

\big|\mathbf{H}^{-1}(z)-\widehat{\Pi}(z)\big|\,\Big|\,\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}\prec\sqrt{\frac{\Im q(z)}{n_{1}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

uniformly for
z\in D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. Finally, integrating with respect to the distribution of
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
, we conclude that

\big|\mathbf{H}^{-1}(z)-\widehat{\Pi}(z)\big|\prec\sqrt{\frac{\Im q(z)}{n_{1}\eta}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

uniformly in
D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
.

The averaged local law in part (ii) follows directly from the averaged version of Theorem 3.14 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
together with analogous arguments.

To show part (iii), we use Theorem
S.6.1
. We only need to consider the second and third diagonal blocks, since the first block is such that
|\hat{q}(z)-q(z)|\prec 1/(n_{2}\eta)
(note that
n_{1}\asymp n_{2}
). As explained in Section
S.7.2
, these two blocks in
\widehat{\Pi}(z)
are linear combinations of the blocks in
\mathbf{J}(-\hat{q}(z))
. Accordingly, the two diagonal blocks in
\Pi(z)
are the same linear combinations of the blocks in
\Omega(-q(z))
. Given Theorem
S.6.1
, to show the concentration of
\widehat{\Pi}(z)
around
\Pi(z)
, it suffices to verify that
-\hat{q}(z)
is away from the support of
\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}
with high probability. By part (2) of Lemma
S.5.1
, there exits a constant
c^{\prime}>0
such that

\inf_{\tau\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda})}|\tau+q(z)|\geq c^{\prime}

for all
z\in D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. Therefore,
\inf_{\tau\in\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda})}|\tau+\hat{q}(z)|\geq c^{\prime}
with high probability. It completes the proof.
∎

The last step in the proof of Theorem
2.2
is to extend the results in Theorem
S.7.3
to non-Gaussianity. We aim to establish the following results.

Theorem S.7.4
.

Suppose that Conditions
C1
–
C6
hold. Fix any
\lambda>0
. For any
a^{\prime}>0
, there exist constants
a>0
such that the following results hold.

(i)

(Entrywise local law) We have

\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)-\Pi_{\lambda}(z)=O_{\prec}(\Phi(z)),

uniformly in
{D}(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
.

(ii)

(Averaged local law) We have

|\overline{L}(z)-{q}_{\lambda}(z)|\prec\frac{1}{n_{1}\eta},

uniformly in
D(a,a^{\prime},n_{1})
. Here
\overline{L}(z)
is the averaged trace of
\mathbf{L}_{\lambda}(z)=(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z))_{11}
.

Once Theorem
S.7.4
is established, it is standard in RMT to deduce Lemma
S.7.1
(edge rigidity) and Theorem
S.7.2
(the Green function comparison theorem). Consequently, edge universality for
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda}
follows, completing the proof of Theorem
2.2
. In particular, given Theorem
S.7.3
, the proof of Lemma
S.7.1
is very similar to that of Theorem
S.6.2
. Such arguments are standard in the literature; see, for example, Lemma 4 of
Han et al. (2016)
and Lemma 1 of
Han et al. (2018)
. We therefore omit the details.

Given Theorem
S.7.3
, Theorem
S.7.4
, and Lemma
S.7.1
, it is likewise routine in RMT to derive the bound required for the Green function comparison theorem. Similar arguments can be found in Section 6 of
Erdős et al. (2012)
and Section 12 of
Han et al. (2018)
. In particular, owing to the close similarity between our linearization matrix
\mathbf{H}(z)
and that in
Han et al. (2018)
, the arguments in Section 12 of
Han et al. (2018)
can be applied essentially verbatim. We therefore omit the details.

The proof of Theorem
S.7.4
is technically considerably more demanding. Nevertheless, closely related analyses have been carried out in Sections 9.1, 9.2, and 10 of
Han et al. (2016)
and
Han et al. (2018)
, by adapting the arguments originally developed in
Knowles and Yin (2017)
. In particular, we have already employed these arguments in Section
S.6.4
to establish the local laws for
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
under non-Gaussianity.

Recall that our linearization matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}
coincides with that of
Han et al. (2018)
in the special case
\lambda=0
. Owing to this structural similarity, most of the arguments in
Han et al. (2018)
can be directly adapted to our setting. We therefore focus only on describing the similarities and differences, and on identifying the modifications required to tailor those arguments to the present context. With these modifications, Theorem
S.7.4
is proved using arguments in Sections 9.1, 9.2 and 10 of
Han et al. (2018)
in a verbatim manner.

For any deterministic unit vector
v
and
w
, any observation matrix
\mathbf{{Z}}
satisfying
C2
, and
z\in D
, define the inner product function

F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}})=F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}},z,v,w)=|v^{T}(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}},z)-\Pi_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}},z))w|.

To show Theorem
S.7.4
, it suffices to show that
F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}})\prec\Phi
for
z\in D
. While this argument is established in Theorem
S.7.3
under Gaussian observations, say
\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}
, we only need to control the difference

\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}})-\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0}).

To this end,
Knowles and Yin (2017)
employ an interpolation method in which a family of distributions
\{f_{\theta}:\theta\in[0,1]\}
is introduced such that
f_{0}
is
N(0,1)
and
f_{1}
is the distribution of the entries of
\mathbf{{Z}}
(see Definition 7.6 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
for details). Let
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
denote the matrix whose entries follow the distribution
f_{\theta}
.

To control
\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{1})-\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0})
, one analyzes the change in
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
induced by replacing a single entry of
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
. Specifically, let
\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,s}
denote the matrix obtained from
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
by replacing its
(u,v)
-th entry with the value
s
. It turns out that, to control
\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{1})-\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{0})
, we only need to control

\sum_{uv}\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbb{E}F_{\lambda}^{k}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}),

for arbitrary power
k\in 2\mathbb{N}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
, and
(u,v)
. See Lemmas 7.9 and 7.10 of
Knowles and Yin (2017)
for details. The analysis relies on explicitly quantifying the difference in
\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})
induced by the change of the
(u,v)
-th entry of
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
. Since
A^{-1}-B^{-1}=-A^{-1}(A-B)B^{-1}
, we have

\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})[\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})]\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}}).

\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})=-(z_{uv}^{1}-z_{uv}^{0})[e_{(n_{1}+p+n_{2})u}e_{n_{0}v}^{T}\Delta^{T}+\Delta e_{n_{0}v}e_{(n_{1}+p+n_{2})u}^{T}].

(S.7.5)

Here,
e_{pi}
is the standard unit vector of dimension
p
in the coordinate direction
i
and

\Delta=\begin{pmatrix}U_{1}^{T}\\[4.0pt]
0_{p\times n_{0}}\\[4.0pt]
U_{2}^{T}\end{pmatrix}.

Arguments in Sections 9.1, 9.2 and 10 of
Han et al. (2018)
are devoted to the study of the above quantities when
\lambda=0
.

The similarities between our setting and that of
Han et al. (2018)
can be summarized as follows. First, the bounds on the norms
\|\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(z)\|
,
\|\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(z)\|
, and
\|\partial_{z}\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}^{-1}(z)\|
by the imaginary part of
z
are unaffected by the presence of the parameter
\lambda
. In particular, the estimates in (9.16)–(9.19) of
Han et al. (2018)
remain valid for all
\lambda>0
. Second, bounds of the form
|v^{T}(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{0}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})-\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z))w|
, such as those established in Lemma 8 of
Han et al. (2018)
, continue to hold for
|v^{T}(\mathbf{H}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta})-\Pi_{\lambda}(z))w|
. Third, the analysis of the perturbation induced by modifying a single entry of
\mathbf{{Z}}^{\theta}
can be carried out in exactly the same manner as in
Han et al. (2018)
. In particular, equation (
S.7.5
) does not depend on
\lambda
, since in the difference
\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{1}})-\mathbf{H}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{{Z}}_{(uv)}^{\theta,z_{uv}^{0}})
the middle block cancels.

The main difference between our setting and that of
Han et al. (2018)
lies in the treatment of the deterministic equivalent matrix. In our context, concentration is established with respect to the deterministic equivalent
\Pi_{\lambda}(z)
, whereas
Han et al. (2018)
employ a
\mathbf{{Z}}U_{2}
–dependent equivalent
\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z)
. This distinction leads to a simpler analysis in our case. Indeed, when individual entries of
\mathbf{{Z}}
are modified, the matrix
\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z)
changes accordingly, while
\Pi_{\lambda}(z)
remains unaffected. Consequently, all arguments in
Han et al. (2018)
that pertain to the analysis of
\widehat{\Pi}_{0}(z)
can be omitted in our setting. These include, for example, the definition of the groups
g^{(j)}
in Section 9.1.2, the second term on the right-hand side of equation (9.42), the quantities
\mathcal{H}_{sti}
and
\mathcal{H}_{stu}
appearing between equations (9.48) and (9.49), equation (9.53), as well as the discussion from the fourth paragraph of Section 9.2 up to equation (9.60), among others.

With these modifications in place, the remaining arguments of
Han et al. (2018)
can be applied in a verbatim manner. We therefore omit further details.
∎

S.8
Proof of Theorem
3.2
, Lemma
3.3
, and Lemma
3.4

First, consider the proof of Theorem
3.2
. Let
F^{\Sigma_{p}}_{(p)}
be the discretization of
F^{\Sigma_{p}}
onto the points in
R_{p}
. Specifically, we assume that
F^{\Sigma_{p}}_{(p)}
is the distribution that places masses
F^{\Sigma_{p}}((\sigma_{p,j-1},\sigma_{pj}])
at the point
\sigma_{pj}
,
j=1,2,\dots
, and
\sigma_{p0}=-\infty
. Since
\mbox{size}(R_{p})=o(p^{-2/3})
, clearly

\mathcal{D}_{W}(F^{\Sigma_{p}}_{(p)},F^{\Sigma_{p}})=o(p^{-2/3}).

Recall the errors in Algorithm
1
as

e_{ij}=\frac{\hat{Q}_{j}(z_{i})}{|\hat{Q}_{j}(z_{i})|}-\frac{1}{|\hat{Q}_{j}(z_{i})|}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\frac{\sigma_{k}^{j}w_{k}}{(\sigma_{k}\lambda\hat{\varphi}(z_{i})+\lambda)^{j}}.

By the convergence of
\hat{\varphi}(z)
, uniformly for
y\in J_{p}
,
\hat{\varphi}(y)-\varphi(y)=o_{P}(n^{-2/3})
and
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(y)-\varphi^{\prime}(y)=o_{P}(n^{-2/3})
. Therefore, together with Marčenko-Pastur equation (
2.1
),

\sup_{y\in J_{p}}\left|\frac{\hat{Q}(y)}{|\hat{Q}(y)|}-\frac{1}{|\hat{Q}(y)|}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau\lambda\hat{\varphi}(y)+\lambda}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

It follows then

\sup_{y\in J_{p}}\left|\frac{\hat{Q}(y)}{|\hat{Q}(y)|}-\frac{1}{|\hat{Q}(y)|}\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}_{(p)}(\tau)}{\tau\lambda\hat{\varphi}(y)+\lambda}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

Then, we can conclude that the estimated measure
\hat{F}_{p}
is such that

\sup_{y\in J_{p}}\left|\int\frac{\tau d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{\tau{\varphi}(y)+1}-\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau{\varphi}(y)+1}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

Under the assumption
\{\varphi(y),y\in J_{p}\}
is dense in
D(\nu)
. Therefore,

\sup_{z\in D(\nu)}\left|\int\frac{\tau d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{\tau z+1}-\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau z+1}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

(S.8.1)

Similarly, we can get

\sup_{z\in D(\nu)}\left|\int\frac{\tau^{2}d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{(\tau z+1)^{2}}-\int\frac{\tau^{2}dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{(\tau z+1)^{2}}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

(S.8.2)

Notice that the functions are analytic in
z
. Consider the power series expansion

\displaystyle\int\frac{\tau d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{\tau z+1}-\int\frac{\tau dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\tau z+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}z^{m}\left(\int\tau^{m+1}(d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)-dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{m}z^{m}(\mu_{m+1}(\hat{F}_{p})-\mu_{m+1}(F^{\Sigma_{p}})).

Here,
\mu_{m}(\cdot)
is the
m
th moment of a distribution. Using Cauchy’s estimates on the power series coefficients, we have

\delta_{p}=\sup_{m\geq 1}p^{2/3}\nu^{m}|\mu_{m}(\hat{F}_{p})-\mu_{m}(F^{\Sigma_{p}})|=o_{P}(1).

Then, it follows that the characteristic functions of
\hat{F}_{p}
and
F^{\Sigma_{p}}
are such that for
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{F}_{p}}e^{itX}-\mathbb{E}_{F^{\Sigma_{p}}}e^{itX}}{t}\right|\leq C\nu^{-1}\delta_{p}p^{-2/3}e^{t/\nu},

\left|\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{F}_{p}}e^{itX}-\mathbb{E}_{F^{\Sigma_{p}}}e^{itX}}{t}\right|\leq C\nu^{-2}\delta_{p}p^{-2/3}e^{t/\nu}.

Following from Corollary 8.3 of
Bobkov (2016)
, for all
T\geq 3

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{W}(\hat{F}_{p},F^{\Sigma_{p}})\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int_{-T}^{T}\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{F}_{p}}e^{itX}-\mathbb{E}_{F^{\Sigma_{p}}}e^{itX}}{t}\right|^{2}dt\right)^{1/2}+\left(\left|\frac{d}{dt}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\hat{F}_{p}}e^{itX}-\mathbb{E}_{F^{\Sigma_{p}}}e^{itX}}{t}\right|^{2}dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\frac{C}{T}.

It follows then

\mathcal{D}_{W}(\hat{F}_{p},F^{\Sigma_{p}})\leq\frac{C(1+\nu)}{\nu^{2}}p^{-2/3}\exp(T)\delta_{p}+\frac{C}{T}.

Note that setting
T=\log(p^{1/3})
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{W}(\hat{F}_{p},F^{\Sigma_{p}})=o_{P}(1).

Next, we consider the scenario when
J_{p}
is such that
\{\varphi(y):~y\in J_{p}\}
eventually covers
[-h_{\beta}/\lambda-\varepsilon,-h_{\beta}/\lambda+\varepsilon]
. Again, due to the fact that uniformly for
y\in J_{p}
,
\hat{\varphi}(y_{p})-\varphi(y_{p})=o_{P}(p^{-2/3})
,
\hat{\varphi}^{\prime}(y_{p})-\varphi^{\prime}(y_{p})=o_{P}(p^{-2/3})
, Eq. (
S.8.1
) and Eq. (
S.8.2
),

\sup_{h\in[h_{\beta}-\lambda\varepsilon,h_{\beta}+\lambda\varepsilon]}\left|\int\frac{\tau d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{-h\tau+\lambda}-\int\frac{\tau d{F}^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{-h\tau+\lambda}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

\sup_{h\in[h_{\beta}-\lambda\varepsilon,h_{\beta}+\lambda\varepsilon]}\left|\int\frac{\tau^{2}d\hat{F}_{p}(\tau)}{(-h\tau+\lambda)^{2}}-\int\frac{\tau^{2}d{F}^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{(-h\tau+\lambda)^{2}}\right|=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}).

It indicates that uniformly in
h\in[h_{\beta}-\lambda\varepsilon,h_{\beta}+\lambda\varepsilon]
,

\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(h)-\mathcal{H}_{1}(h)=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}),\quad\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{2}(h)-\mathcal{H}_{2}(h)=o_{P}(p^{-2/3})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}(h)-\mathcal{H}_{3}(h)=o_{P}(1).

Here, the bound on
\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{3}(h)-\mathcal{H}_{3}(h)
is due to
\mathcal{D}_{W}(\hat{F}_{p},F^{\Sigma_{p}})
. As
x
,
s(x)
,
s^{\prime}(x)
, and
s^{\prime\prime}(x)
are smooth in
\mathcal{H}_{j}(h)
,
j=1,2,3
as shown in Theorem
3.1
, we get

\hat{\beta}-\beta=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}),\quad\hat{s}(\hat{\beta})-s(\beta)=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}),

\hat{s}^{\prime}(\hat{\beta})-s^{\prime}(\beta)=o_{P}(p^{-2/3}),\quad\hat{s}^{\prime\prime}(\hat{\beta})-s^{\prime\prime}(\beta)=o_{P}(1).

Consequently,
\hat{\Theta}_{1}-\Theta_{1}=o_{P}(p^{-2/3})
and
\hat{\Theta}_{2}-\Theta_{2}=o_{P}(1)
.

As for Lemma
3.3
, recall that
\varphi(z)
is the Stieltjes transform of
\mathcal{W}
. Then,

\varphi(z)=\int\frac{d\mathcal{W}}{\tau-z}.

It follows that
\{\varphi(z):z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}~\mbox{and}~\operatorname{dist}(z,\operatorname{supp}(\mathcal{W}))\geq c\}
contains the half disk
\mathcal{D}(\nu)
for some
\nu>0
. We can then select
J_{p}
such that
\varphi(y)
is dense in
\mathcal{D}(\nu)
when
y\in J_{p}
.

As for Lemma
3.4
, as
\hat{\gamma}_{1}\to\infty
,
\beta\to 0
and
h_{\beta}/\lambda\to-\varphi(-\lambda)
. Therefore, select
J_{p}
to be a grid containing a subsequence dense in
[-3/2\lambda,-\lambda/2]
. Then,
\{\varphi(y):y\in J_{p}\}
will eventually covers a neighborhood of
-h_{\beta}/\lambda
.

S.9
Proof of Lemma
4.1
, Corollary
4.1
, Lemma
4.2
and Corollary
4.2

First of all, under
H_{a}:BC\neq 0
, we can decompose
\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}
as

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}^{(0)}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}BC(C^{T}(XX^{T})^{-1}C)^{-1}C^{T}B^{T}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n_{1}}\left(BXP_{1}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}\Sigma^{1/2}_{p}+\Sigma_{p}^{1/2}\mathbf{{Z}}P_{1}X^{T}B^{T}\right)(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1},

where
\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}^{(0)}
is the regularized
F
-matrix under the null hypothesis
H_{0}:BC=0
.

Therefore, under
DA
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p^{s}}\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p^{s}}\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}^{(0)}+d_{p}q_{p}q_{p}^{T}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n_{1}p^{s}}\left(BXP_{1}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}\Sigma^{1/2}_{p}+\Sigma_{p}^{1/2}\mathbf{{Z}}P_{1}X^{T}B^{T}\right)(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}.

Since
\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda}^{(0)})=O_{P}(1)
as indicated by Theorem
2.2
and

\frac{1}{p^{s/2}n_{1}}\|BXP_{1}\|_{2}\|\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}\|\|\Sigma_{p}^{1/2}\|\|(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}\|=O_{P}(1),

we have

\frac{1}{p^{s}}\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda})=d_{p}q_{p}^{T}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}q_{p}+o_{P}(1).

Using the main results of
El Karoui and Kösters (2011)
, we can show that

q_{p}^{T}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}q_{p}=q_{p}^{T}(\lambda\varphi(-\lambda)\Sigma_{p}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}q_{p}+o_{P}(1).

The result of Lemma
4.1
follows.

As for Corollary
4.1
, under the assumed conditions,
\tilde{\ell}(\lambda)\to\infty
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(\lambda)>\mathbb{TW}_{1}(1-\alpha)\mid BC\Big)\longrightarrow 1.

Under
PA
, similarly, we get
p^{-s}\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda})
is dominated by the following term

\frac{1}{p^{s}}\ell_{\max}(\mathbf{F}_{\lambda})=\frac{1}{p}\nu^{T}D^{1/2}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}D^{1/2}\nu+o_{P}(1).

Using Lemma 2.7 of
Bai and Silverstein (1998)
, conditional on
\mathbf{W}_{2}
,

\frac{1}{p}\nu^{T}D^{1/2}(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}D^{1/2}\nu=\frac{1}{p}\mathrm{tr}\Big[(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}D\Big]+o_{P}(1).

Here, the residual
o_{P}(1)
is with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{BC}
and the convergence is uniform with respect to
\mathbf{W}_{2}
since
\|(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}\|_{2}\leq 1/\lambda
for any
\mathbf{W}_{2}
.

Again, using the main results of
El Karoui and Kösters (2011)
,

\frac{1}{p}\mathrm{tr}\Big[(\mathbf{W}_{2}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}D\Big]=\frac{1}{p}\mathrm{tr}\Big[(\lambda\varphi(-\lambda)\Sigma_{p}+\lambda I_{p})^{-1}D\Big]+o_{P}(1).

The results of Lemma
4.2
follow.

As for Corollary
4.2
, under the assumed conditions,
\tilde{\ell}(\lambda)\to\infty
. Therefore,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\tilde{\ell}(\lambda)>\mathbb{TW}_{1}(1-\alpha)\mid BC\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathbb{P}_{BC}}}{{\longrightarrow}}1.

S.10
Potential relaxation of technical conditions

The technical assumptions imposed in this work may be further relaxed. In this section, we briefly discuss possible directions for such relaxation.

First, in Condition
C1
, convergence of
\hat{\gamma}_{1}=p/n_{1}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{2}=p/n_{2}
to
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
is assumed. This requirement may be relaxed by replacing convergence with mere boundedness of
\hat{\gamma}_{1}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{2}
in the sense that

Boundedness of aspect ratios.

There exist constants
c,C>0
such that
c<\hat{\gamma}_{1}<C
and
c<\hat{\gamma}_{2}<C
, for all sufficiently large
p
.

Such a formulation has been adopted in the RMT literature; see, for example,
Ding et al. (2024)
. In fact, under this weaker assumption, no modifications to the arguments in Sections
S.6
,
S.7
,
S.8
, and
S.9
would be required.

Second, on the distributional side, Condition
C2
requires the existence of all moments of
z_{ij}
. For edge universality of
\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda}
, this assumption could potentially be weakened to a fourth-moment–type condition. Indeed,
Ding and Yang (2018)
showed that universality for the sample covariance matrix
n_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{{Z}}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}
holds if and only if

\lim_{t\to\infty}t^{4}\,\mathbb{P}(|z_{ij}|\geq t)=0,

which is slightly weaker than the standard finite fourth-moment condition. In the present setting, we conjecture that universality for

\mathbf{G}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{{Z}}U_{1}U_{1}^{T}\mathbf{{Z}}^{T}+\lambda\Sigma_{p}^{-1}

and for
\tilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\lambda}
could likewise be established under this tail condition. Proving such an extension would require substantial additional technical work and is therefore left for future research.

Third, Conditions
C4
,
C5
, and
C6
may be replaced by the following alternative set of assumptions. Recall that

x_{p}(h)=h+\left[1+\frac{p}{n_{2}}\int\frac{\tau\,dF^{\Sigma_{p}}(\tau)}{\lambda-\tau h}\right]^{-1},

and

f(q)=-\frac{1}{q}+\frac{p}{n_{1}}\int\frac{d\mathcal{G}_{p\lambda}(\tau)}{\tau+q}=-\frac{1}{q}+\hat{\gamma}_{1}\,\phi_{p\lambda}(-q).

Regularity of the population spectrum.

There exists a constant
c>0
such that, for all sufficiently large
p
, the following conditions (i) and (ii) hold:

(i)

One of the following alternatives is satisfied:

(a)

|\lambda-\sigma_{1p}h_{0}|\geq c
, where
h_{0}
is the unique solution of
x_{p}^{\prime}(h_{0})=0
in
(-\infty,\lambda/\sigma_{1p})
. In this case, define
\rho_{p\lambda}=x_{p}(h_{0})
.

(b)

(p/n_{2})\,F^{\Sigma_{p}}(\{\sigma_{1p}\})>1+c
. In this case, define
\rho_{p\lambda}=\lambda/\sigma_{1p}
.

(ii)

|\beta-\rho_{p\lambda}|\geq c
, where
\beta
satisfies
f^{\prime}(-\beta)=0
for
\beta\in(-\infty,\rho_{p\lambda})
.

Conditions of this form are used in studies of edge universality for random matrices in the literature; see, for example, Assumption 2.5 of
Ding and Yang (2018)
.

S.11
Extension to more general linear hypotheses

In this section, we discuss how the proposed method can be extended from the linear hypothesis
H_{0}:BC=0
to the more general form

H_{0}:ABC=\Gamma\quad\text{against}\quad H_{a}:ABC\neq\Gamma,

(S.11.1)

where
A
is a given
p_{0}\times p
matrix of rank
p_{0}
,
C
is a given
m\times n_{1}
matrix of rank
n_{1}
, and
\Gamma
is a given
p_{0}\times n_{1}
matrix.

The proposed method can be adapted to accommodate this hypothesis. Recall the linear model

\mathbf{Y}=BX+\Sigma_{p}^{1/2}\mathbf{{Z}}.

Define the transformed response matrix

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}=A\mathbf{Y}.

Then

\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}=\tilde{B}X+\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}^{1/2}\mathbf{{Z}},

where
\tilde{B}=AB
and
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}=A\Sigma_{p}A^{T}
. Under this transformation, the hypothesis (
S.11.1
) is equivalent to

H_{0}:\tilde{B}C=\Gamma\quad\text{against}\quad H_{a}:\tilde{B}C\neq\Gamma.

Define the matrices

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\left[\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}X^{T}(XX^{T})^{-1}C-\Gamma\right]\left[C^{T}(XX^{T})^{-1}C\right]^{-1}\left[C^{T}(XX^{T})^{-1}X\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{T}-\Gamma^{T}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{2}^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{2}}\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}\left[I-X^{T}(XX^{T})^{-1}X\right]\tilde{\mathbf{Y}}^{T},

with
n_{1}
being the rank of
C
and
n_{2}=n_{0}-m
being the effective sample size.
The proposed procedure can then be applied to the regularized
F
-matrix

\mathbf{W}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{W}_{2}^{*}+\lambda I_{p_{0}})^{-1}.

Here, it is understood that
p
and
\Sigma_{p}
are replaced by
p_{0}
and
\tilde{\Sigma}_{p}
. The theoretical results continue to hold provided that
(p_{0},n_{1},n_{2},\tilde{\Sigma}_{p})
satisfy Conditions
C1
–
C6
.
